Make true/false statements about yourself, then correct the false ones with negatives.

Common pitfall : Only ONE negation and the verb stays base: 'He doesn't work' (not 'doesn't works', not 'don't never').

Adverbs of frequency (always, often, never) A140 questions

Grammar note

The main ones are always, usually, often, sometimes, rarely, never. They go before a main verb (I often walk), but after be (He is often late) and after an auxiliary (I have never been there). Sometimes and usually may also open or close the sentence.

  • She always arrives early. — before a main verb
  • They are never at home on Sunday. — after be
  • I don’t usually eat breakfast. — after the auxiliary
  • Sometimes we go to the beach. — front position
Teaching sequence
1
A frequency scale

Order always → usually → often → sometimes → rarely → never on a line. Link to routines.

Support : 'I always have breakfast.'

2
Position

Mid-position: before the main verb but after 'be'. 'She often works late' / 'She is often late.'

3
Production

Class survey on habits; report frequencies.

Common pitfall : With 'be' the adverb goes AFTER ('is always'), with other verbs BEFORE ('always goes'). 'Never' is already negative — don't add 'not'.

Syntax & discourse

Word order (SVO) A140 questions

Grammar note

An English statement follows subject + verb + object: Anna eats an apple. Nothing is placed between the verb and its object; adverbs and phrases of place and time come after it, place normally before time: I saw a film at the cinema last night.

  • The dog chased the cat. — subject, verb, object
  • The cat chased the dog. — same words, opposite meaning
  • We study English every day. — time phrase at the end
  • *I like very much this song → I like this song very much
Teaching sequence
1
Subject-Verb-Object

Establish the fixed S-V-O order with colour-coded cards. English relies on order, not case endings.

Support : 'The cat (S) eats (V) the fish (O).'

2
Manipulate

Rebuild scrambled sentences; show how changing order changes meaning ('The dog bites the man' is not the reverse).

3
Production

Write simple S-V-O sentences from pictures.

Common pitfall : Word order carries the meaning, so it is rigid: the doer comes first. There are no free word-order options at A1.

Yes/no questions (do / does, be) A140 questions

Grammar note

With be, simply put the verb before the subject: Are you ready? With all other verbs, begin with do or does + subject + base verb: Do they live here? Does she work? The -s sits on does, so the main verb has none. Short answers repeat the auxiliary.

  • Is he a doctor? — Yes, he is. — be is inverted
  • Do you speak Spanish? — No, I don’t.
  • Does your brother play football? — third person singular
  • *Does she works here? → Does she work here?
Teaching sequence
1
Two question types

With 'be'/modals, invert (Are you...? Can you...?); with other verbs, add Do/Does at the front.

Support : 'Do you like tea? / Are you tired?'

2
Short answers

Drill 'Yes, I do / No, I don't', 'Yes, she is / No, she isn't'. Match the auxiliary.

3
Production

Find-someone-who survey using yes/no questions.

Common pitfall : Use the matching auxiliary in short answers ('Do you...? → Yes, I do', not 'Yes, I like'). Don't double the subject.

Wh- questions (what, where, when, who) A140 questions

Grammar note

Put the wh- word first, then build a yes/no question after it: wh- + do/does/be + subject + verb. What do you want? Where is she? When the wh- word is itself the subject (who, what), no auxiliary is added and the order stays normal: Who lives here?

  • Where do you work? — auxiliary do is kept
  • What is your name? — be is inverted
  • When does the film start? — does + base verb
  • Who wants a coffee? — wh- word is the subject, no do
Teaching sequence
1
Question word + auxiliary

Wh- word first, then the yes/no question structure: 'Where do you live? What is this?'

Support : 'When does the film start?'

2
who as subject

Note 'Who lives here?' needs no 'do' (who = subject). Brief contrast with object questions.

3
Production

Interview a partner with a set of wh- questions.

Common pitfall : Keep the auxiliary and word order: 'Where do you work?' not 'Where you work?' or 'Where do you working?'.

Basic connectors (and, but, or, because) A140 questions

Grammar note

And adds, but contrasts, or gives an alternative, because gives a reason. All four can join two complete clauses, each with its own subject and verb: I stayed at home because I was ill. And, but and or can also link single words or phrases.

  • I like tea and coffee. — linking two nouns
  • She is small but very strong. — contrast
  • Do you want to walk or take the bus? — alternative
  • We left early because the train was at six. — reason
Teaching sequence
1
Join ideas

and (addition), but (contrast), or (choice), because (reason). Combine short sentences.

Support : 'I'm tired but happy.'

2
because = reason

Answer 'why?' with 'because + clause'. Drill cause-reason pairs.

3
Production

Write three linked sentences about your day using all four connectors.

Common pitfall : 'Because' introduces a full clause (subject + verb): 'because it was late', not 'because of late' (that's 'because of + noun').

Number & quantity

Cardinal numbers (0–100) A140 questions

Grammar note

One to twelve are separate words; 13 to 19 end in -teen (thirteen, fifteen, eighteen); the tens end in -ty (twenty, thirty, forty, fifty, sixty, seventy, eighty, ninety). In between, join tens and units with a hyphen: twenty-one, forty-six. 100 is a hundred.

  • 13 thirteen / 30 thirty — teen against ty
  • 40 forty — no u, unlike four
  • 58 fifty-eight — tens plus units, with a hyphen
  • 100 a hundred, or one hundred in counting
Teaching sequence
1
Build in steps

0–12 (irregular), 13–19 (-teen), 20–90 (-ty), then combine (twenty-one). Listening practice for -teen vs -ty.

Support : 'thirteen' (long ee) vs 'thirty' (short i)

2
Hyphen & 'and'

Write compound numbers with a hyphen (forty-five). Note BrE 'a hundred and five'.

3
Production

Exchange phone numbers, prices and ages aloud.

Common pitfall : -teen vs -ty (fourteen/forty) is the classic confusion — stress and the final sound differ. Drill minimal pairs.

some / any A140 questions

Grammar note

Some and any stand before plural or uncountable nouns and mean an unspecified quantity. Use some in positive statements (I have some questions) and any in negatives and questions (I haven’t got any money). Some also appears in offers and requests: Would you like some tea?

  • There is some milk in the fridge. — positive, uncountable
  • We didn’t buy any vegetables. — negative, plural
  • Are there any messages for me? — question
  • Could I have some water, please? — request
Teaching sequence
1
Affirmative vs Q/neg

'some' in positive sentences, 'any' in questions and negatives. Use with plurals and uncountables.

Support : 'I have some bread. / Is there any milk?'

2
Offers & requests

Note 'some' in offers/requests ('Would you like some tea?'). Quick exception.

3
Production

Talk about what's in the fridge using some/any.

Common pitfall : Default 'any' for questions and negatives ('I don't have any money'), but use 'some' when offering or requesting.

A2 A2 — Elementary

Everyday situations · describing · simple narration

Nouns & articles

Possessive 's (Saxon genitive) A240 questions

Grammar note

Add ’s to a singular noun: my sister’s car, the dog’s name. A plural already ending in -s takes the apostrophe alone: my parents’ house. Irregular plurals take ’s: the children’s toys. The possessor comes first, the thing possessed second.

  • Peter’s phone is broken. — singular owner
  • The students’ results — plural owner ending in -s
  • The women’s team — irregular plural
  • Today’s newspaper — time expressions take ’s too
Teaching sequence
1
's for people

Use 's to show possession, mainly with people and animals: 'Tom's car', 'the dog's tail'.

Support : 'my sister's friend'

2
Singular vs plural

Singular noun + 's; plural noun ending in -s + apostrophe only (the students' books). Contrast drill.

3
Production

Describe who owns what in a family tree.

Common pitfall : For things, prefer 'of': 'the door of the car', not 'the car's door'. Reserve 's mainly for living possessors.

Zero article (general plurals & uncountables) A240 questions

Grammar note

Use no article before plural countable nouns and before uncountable nouns when you speak in general: Dogs are loyal. Water boils at 100 degrees. Zero article is also normal with most countries, languages, meals, sports and abstract nouns.

  • Cats sleep a lot. — general plural
  • Cheese is made from milk. — uncountable, general
  • He speaks Japanese and teaches history.
  • We have dinner at eight. — meals take no article
Teaching sequence
1
No article for generalisations

Drop 'the' when talking about things in general: 'I like dogs / Coffee is expensive.'

Support : 'Children need sleep.'

2
Specific vs general

Contrast 'Dogs are friendly' (general, no article) with 'The dogs next door bark' (specific). Sorting task.

3
Production

State general likes/dislikes with zero article.

Common pitfall : Adding 'the' to a generalisation changes the meaning to a specific group: 'I love the music' (a particular one) vs 'I love music' (in general).

Verbs & tenses

Past simple (regular -ed) A240 questions

Grammar note

Add -ed to the base verb; the form is the same for every person: I worked, she worked. Spelling: a final -e takes only -d (live → lived); consonant + y becomes -ied (study → studied); a single short stressed vowel doubles the consonant (stop → stopped). Use it for finished actions at a definite past time.

  • She walked to school yesterday. — one form for all persons
  • They carried the boxes upstairs. — consonant + y becomes -ied
  • We stopped at the first café. — doubled final consonant
  • He wanted to help. — the -ed here adds a syllable: want-ed
Teaching sequence
1
Three -ed sounds

Teach the endings /t/ (worked), /d/ (played), /id/ (wanted). Listening discrimination.

Support : 'wanted' adds a syllable /id/

2
Q & neg with did

Questions/negatives use did/didn't + base verb (no -ed): 'Did you work? / I didn't work.'

3
Production

Narrate last weekend with regular past verbs.

Common pitfall : After did/didn't the verb is the base form, not the past: 'Did you go?' not 'Did you went?'. The past marker moves to 'did'.

Past simple (irregular verbs) A240 questions

Grammar note

Many frequent verbs make the past with a vowel change or a completely different word instead of -ed: go → went, see → saw, take → took, buy → bought, come → came, have → had. Some do not change at all: put, cut, let. The form is identical for every person, and be is the only verb with two forms, was and were.

  • I went to Rome last summer. — go → went
  • She bought a new phone on Friday. — buy → bought
  • They put the bags down and left. — put → put, no change
  • He was tired, but we were fine. — be: was / were
Teaching sequence
1
High-frequency list

Drill the most common irregulars (go/went, have/had, see/saw, make/made, take/took). Group by pattern where possible.

2
Same Q/neg rule

Questions and negatives still use did/didn't + base verb, so the irregular form appears only in affirmatives.

Support : 'I went' but 'Did you go?'

3
Production

Tell a short story of a past trip mixing regular and irregular verbs.

Common pitfall : Irregular forms drop out in questions/negatives: 'I saw it' but 'Did you see it?' / 'I didn't see it' (never 'didn't saw').

Past continuous A240 questions

Grammar note

Form: was/were + verb-ing (I/he/she/it was, you/we/they were). Use it for an action in progress at a past moment, for a longer background action interrupted by a shorter one, usually with when, and for two actions running side by side, usually with while. Negative: wasn’t/weren’t + -ing.

  • At eight o’clock I was having dinner. — in progress at a past moment
  • She was reading when the lights went out. — long action interrupted
  • While we were waiting, they were playing cards. — two parallel actions
  • They weren’t listening to me. — negative form
Teaching sequence
1
was/were + -ing

Action in progress in the past: 'At 8 p.m. I was watching TV.' Timeline work.

Support : 'They were sleeping.'

2
Interrupted action

Combine with past simple: 'I was cooking when the phone rang.' Past continuous = background, past simple = interruption.

3
Production

Describe what people were doing during an event (e.g. a power cut).

Common pitfall : Use past simple, not continuous, for the completed/interrupting action: 'when the phone rang' (not 'was ringing' for the single event).

'going to' future (plans & predictions) A240 questions

Grammar note

Form: am/is/are + going to + base verb. Use it for a plan or intention decided before the moment of speaking, and for a prediction based on evidence you can see right now. Negative: I’m not going to…; question: Are you going to…? Note the fixed to, which never disappears.

  • We’re going to move house in June. — plan made before now
  • Look at those clouds! It’s going to rain. — prediction from evidence
  • She isn’t going to take the job. — negative form
  • Are you going to call him tonight? — question form
Teaching sequence
1
be going to + verb

Intentions and plans already decided: 'I'm going to study tonight.' Drill the form.

Support : 'We're going to travel in July.'

2
Evidence predictions

Also for predictions based on what we see now: 'Look at the clouds — it's going to rain.'

3
Production

Talk about weekend plans using 'going to'.

Common pitfall : 'Going to' = plan/intention or evidence-based prediction; don't use it for instant decisions made while speaking (that's 'will').

'will' future (decisions & predictions) A240 questions

Grammar note

Form: will + bare infinitive, the same for every person, contracted to ’ll and won’t; questions are made by inversion, with no do. Use it for a decision taken while you speak, for offers and promises, and for predictions and opinions, often after I think, I’m sure or probably.

  • I think it will rain tomorrow. — prediction or opinion
  • That bag looks heavy. I’ll carry it. — decision taken now, and an offer
  • She won’t be late, I promise. — negative and promise
  • Will you help me with this? — question, no does or do
Teaching sequence
1
Instant decisions

Use 'will' for decisions made at the moment of speaking and offers: 'I'll help you. / I'll have the soup.'

2
Predictions/opinions

For opinions about the future after think/probably: 'I think it will rain.' Contrast with 'going to'.

3
Production

React to situations with spontaneous 'I'll...' offers.

Common pitfall : 'Will' for spur-of-the-moment decisions; 'going to' for prior plans. 'The phone's ringing — I'll get it' (decision now), not 'I'm going to get it'.

Present perfect (experience & recent past) A240 questions

Grammar note

Form: have/has + past participle (worked, seen, been). Use it for experience at an unstated time, with ever and never; for a recent event, with just, already and yet; and for a situation that started in the past and is still true, with for (a period) and since (a starting point).

  • I have never eaten octopus. — experience, no time given
  • She’s just finished her homework. — recent event
  • Have you booked the tickets yet? — question with yet
  • We’ve lived here for ten years, since 2016. — still true now
Teaching sequence
1
have/has + past participle

Link past to now: life experience and recent results. 'I have visited Paris. / She has just arrived.'

Support : 'Have you ever eaten sushi?'

2
ever / never / just / yet

Introduce the typical markers. Contrast with past simple + finished time ('yesterday' → past simple).

3
Production

'Find someone who has ever...' class mingle.

Common pitfall : With a finished time expression (yesterday, last year, in 2010) use the past simple, NOT the present perfect: 'I saw it yesterday' (not 'I have seen it yesterday').

Modals: can / can't (ability & permission) A240 questions

Grammar note

Can is followed by the bare infinitive and is identical for every person. The negative is can’t (cannot), and questions are made by putting can first: Can you swim? Use it for ability, for permission and for polite requests and offers. The past form is could / couldn’t.

  • He can play the guitar. — ability
  • You can’t park here. — something not permitted
  • Can I open the window? — asking permission
  • I couldn’t hear you. — past ability
Teaching sequence
1
can + base verb

Ability and permission: 'I can swim. Can I open the window?' No -s, no 'to'.

Support : 'She can drive.'

2
Negatives & questions

can't / cannot; questions by inversion (Can you...?). Pronunciation of can (weak) vs can't.

3
Production

Talk about skills and ask permission in role-plays.

Common pitfall : Modals take the bare infinitive and never add -s or 'to': 'She can swim', not 'cans' or 'can to swim'.

Modals: must / have to (obligation) A240 questions

Grammar note

Both express obligation and are followed by the base verb. Must is the speaker’s own obligation and never changes: I must go, she must go. Have to points to an outside rule and behaves like an ordinary verb: I have to, she has to, do you have to. Only have to has a past and a future: I had to wait, I’ll have to wait.

  • I must remember to phone her. — the speaker’s own obligation
  • She has to wear a uniform at work. — third person: has to
  • Did you have to pay in advance? — question with did
  • We had to leave early. — past obligation, always had to
Teaching sequence
1
Two ways to say 'obligation'

'must' (speaker's authority/strong) and 'have to' (external rule). 'I must finish this. / I have to wear a uniform.'

2
Negatives differ

'mustn't' = prohibition; 'don't have to' = no obligation. Crucial contrast drill.

Support : 'You mustn't smoke' is not 'You don't have to smoke'

3
Production

List rules at school/work using must / have to.

Common pitfall : 'mustn't' (forbidden) and 'don't have to' (not necessary) mean opposite things — the most common A2 confusion.

Modals: should (advice) A240 questions

Grammar note

Should is followed by the bare infinitive and is the same for every person: you should rest, he should rest. Negative shouldn’t; question by inversion: Should I call her? Use it to give advice, an opinion or a recommendation, and to say what is right or expected. Ought to has the same meaning.

  • You should drink more water. — advice
  • He shouldn’t work so late. — negative advice
  • Should we book a table? — asking for advice
  • Children should be polite to their teachers. — what is expected
Teaching sequence
1
should + base verb

Give advice and recommendations: 'You should rest. You shouldn't worry.'

Support : 'Should I call her?'

2
Soft vs strong

Position 'should' as softer than 'must'. Drill advice for common problems.

3
Production

Agony-aunt activity: give advice with should/shouldn't.

Common pitfall : 'Should' takes the bare infinitive ('You should see a doctor', not 'should to see'); it advises, it doesn't oblige.

Zero conditional (general truths & instructions) A240 questions

Grammar note

If + present simple in the if-clause, present simple in the main clause. It describes a result that is always the same: scientific facts, rules, habits and automatic reactions. The two clauses can change places, and the comma disappears when the if-clause comes second. For instructions, the main clause is an imperative.

  • If you press this button, the machine stops. — automatic result
  • Ice melts if you leave it in the sun. — reversed order, no comma
  • When she is tired, she gets angry. — habitual reaction
  • If the alarm rings, leave the building. — imperative for instructions
Teaching sequence
1
Noticing in context

Hand out three short authentic texts that contain nothing but zero conditionals: a fire-drill notice, the instructions on a coffee machine, and four science facts. Learners underline every verb and answer one question only — what tense is used in each half of the sentence? Elicit the discovery that both clauses are present simple and that [will] appears nowhere.

Support : Support: one-page handout — fire-drill notice, coffee-machine instructions, four science facts.

2
Guided discovery: the if/when test

Put two pairs on the board — [If you press this button, the door opens.] / [When you press this button, the door opens.] against [If it rains, I will stay in.] / [When it rains, I will stay in.]. Ask learners which pair means the same thing twice. Draw the conclusion for them: when the result is automatic, [if] and [when] are interchangeable, and that swap is the test that separates this pattern from the first conditional they already know.

3
Controlled practice and punctuation

Run a substitution drill from a fixed frame — [If you ___ , it ___ .] — feeding in heat ice, drop a glass, forget to water plants. Then make each learner rewrite two sentences with the clauses reversed and correct only the punctuation: comma when the if-clause comes first, no comma when it comes second.

Support : Support: a set of twelve prompt cards, six facts and six machine actions.

4
Freer production: rules and instructions

In pairs, learners write six rules for a place they know — the school lab, a swimming pool, a shared kitchen — using an imperative result clause: [If the alarm rings, leave the building.] Require one sentence with [unless]. Correct two things only: any stray [will] and any missing third-person -s.

Common pitfall : Learners transfer the first conditional wholesale and produce [If you heat water, it will boil]; the mirror-image error is assuming [if] and [when] are always interchangeable, which yields [When it rains, I will stay in] for a single possible occasion. Apply the swap test to every sentence: if [when] leaves the meaning untouched, the result is automatic and both clauses stay in the present simple — otherwise it is a first conditional and needs [will].

Adjectives

Comparatives (-er / more ... than) A240 questions

Grammar note

One-syllable adjectives add -er: taller, faster (big → bigger, nice → nicer). Two-syllable adjectives in -y take -ier: happier, easier. Longer adjectives take more: more expensive, more careful. The second term follows than. Irregular: good → better, bad → worse, far → further.

  • My brother is taller than me. — short adjective + -er
  • This film is more interesting than the book. — long adjective + more
  • Today is hotter than yesterday. — doubled final consonant
  • *more good → better — irregular comparative
Teaching sequence
1
Short vs long adjectives

One syllable + -er (taller); two-plus syllables → more (more expensive). 'than' for the comparison.

Support : 'cheaper than', 'more useful than'

2
Irregulars & spelling

good→better, bad→worse, far→further; double the consonant (big→bigger), -y→-ier (happy→happier).

3
Production

Compare two cities, phones or people.

Common pitfall : Don't combine the two systems: 'more bigger' is wrong. Use either -er OR more, never both.

Superlatives (the -est / the most) A240 questions

Grammar note

Short adjectives take -est: the tallest, the biggest, the easiest. Long adjectives take the most: the most expensive. Irregular: the best, the worst, the furthest. A superlative singles out one member of a group, so it is normally followed by in with places and groups, or of with periods and quantities.

  • She is the youngest in the family. — short adjective, in + group
  • It was the most beautiful day of the year. — the most + of
  • That was the worst film I have ever seen. — irregular form
  • Sunday was the happiest day of my life. — -y becomes -iest
Teaching sequence
1
the + -est / most

Short adjectives + -est (the tallest); long ones the most (the most expensive). Always with 'the'.

Support : 'the best film of the year'

2
Irregulars

good→the best, bad→the worst, far→the furthest. Drill alongside comparatives.

3
Production

Rank a top-three list (films, places) using superlatives.

Common pitfall : Superlatives need 'the' ('the biggest', not just 'biggest') and the same short/long split as comparatives.

Adjective order (opinion-size-age-colour) A240 questions

Grammar note

When several adjectives come before a noun, English keeps a fixed order: opinion, then size, age, shape, colour, origin, material and purpose. No commas and no and inside the chain: a lovely little old round brown Italian wooden table. In real speech two or three adjectives is the usual maximum.

  • a beautiful large modern house — opinion, size, age
  • a small black leather bag — size, colour, material
  • an interesting old French film — opinion, age, origin
  • *a red big ball → a big red ball — size comes before colour
Teaching sequence
1
A rough order

When stacking adjectives: opinion → size → age → shape → colour → origin → material → noun. 'a lovely big old house.'

2
Two is plenty

In real use, two or three adjectives is typical. Order familiar pairs (big red, nice old).

3
Production

Describe objects with two stacked adjectives in the natural order.

Common pitfall : Native order feels 'automatic': 'a big red ball', not 'a red big ball'. Drill the most frequent combinations rather than the full chain.

Pronouns

Reflexive pronouns (myself, yourself...) A240 questions

Grammar note

The forms are myself, yourself, himself, herself, itself, ourselves, yourselves, themselves — note the singular yourself against the plural yourselves. Use one when the subject and the object are the same person, to emphasise who acted, and after by to mean alone, without help.

  • I hurt myself playing football. — subject and object are the same
  • The children dressed themselves. — plural form
  • The manager himself answered the phone. — emphasis
  • She painted the kitchen by herself. — by + reflexive = alone
Teaching sequence
1
Same subject & object

Use myself, yourself, himself... when subject and object are the same: 'I cut myself.'

Support : 'She taught herself Italian.'

2
Emphasis use

Also for emphasis: 'I did it myself.' And 'by myself' = alone. Brief contrast.

3
Production

Talk about things you do for yourself / by yourself.

Common pitfall : Some verbs are not reflexive in English though they are in other languages: 'I feel good' / 'I relax' (not 'myself'). Don't overuse reflexives.

'one' / 'ones' (substitution) A240 questions

Grammar note

To avoid repeating a countable noun already mentioned, replace it with one (singular) or ones (plural). It is used after an adjective (the red one), after this, that, these, those, which, and after the, another, each: I need a bigger one.

  • I like the blue shirt, not the green one. — after an adjective
  • Which biscuits? — The chocolate ones, please. — plural
  • My phone is old, so I bought a new one. — a + adjective + one
  • *Which car? The red. → Which car? The red one.
Teaching sequence
1
Avoid repeating the noun

Use 'one/ones' to replace a countable noun already mentioned: 'I'll take the red one.'

Support : 'Which ones do you like?'

2
With this/that & adjectives

'this one, the big ones, a new one'. Drill substitution in shopping contexts.

3
Production

Shopping role-play choosing between items using one/ones.

Common pitfall : 'One/ones' replaces only countable nouns; it can't replace an uncountable noun ('Which milk? — the fresh one' does not work for milk).

Adverbs & negation

Adverbs of manner (-ly) A240 questions

Grammar note

Adverbs of manner say how an action is done and are normally formed from the adjective + -ly. Watch the spelling: happy → happily, gentle → gently, automatic → automatically. A few keep the adjective form with no -ly: fast, hard, late, early, straight. The adverb usually follows the verb or the object.

  • She drove carefully through the fog. — careful + -ly, after the verb
  • He speaks English fluently. — after the object
  • They arrived early and ran fast. — no -ly ending
  • *He explained it clear → He explained it clearly.
Teaching sequence
1
adjective + -ly

Form manner adverbs from adjectives: quick→quickly, careful→carefully. They say HOW.

Support : 'She sings beautifully.'

2
Irregulars

good→well, fast→fast, hard→hard. Note 'hardly' is not 'hard'. Sorting drill.

3
Production

Describe how people do actions (mime + adverb).

Common pitfall : 'good' is an adjective, 'well' the adverb: 'She sings well' (not 'sings good'). And 'hardly' means 'almost not', not 'with effort'.

too / enough A240 questions

Grammar note

too means more than you want or need: too + adjective or adverb, too much + uncountable, too many + plural. enough means the amount needed: adjective or adverb + enough, but enough + noun. Both are often completed by for + person or by a to-infinitive.

  • This coffee is too hot to drink. — too + adjective + to-infinitive
  • She is not old enough to vote. — adjective + enough
  • We have enough chairs for everyone. — enough + noun
  • There was too much noise and too many cars. — uncountable and plural
Teaching sequence
1
Position differs

'too' before adjective (too small); 'enough' after adjective but before noun (big enough / enough money).

Support : 'It's too expensive. / Is it big enough?'

2
too much/many

'too much' + uncountable, 'too many' + plural. Contrast 'too' (excess) with 'enough' (sufficiency).

3
Production

Complain/comment about a room or meal using too/enough.

Common pitfall : 'enough' follows the adjective ('warm enough') but precedes the noun ('enough time'). 'too' always precedes the adjective.

already / yet / just (with present perfect) A240 questions

Grammar note

just and already go between have/has and the past participle: just marks something finished a moment ago, already something that happened sooner than expected. yet means up to now and closes the clause. Compare still, which keeps a situation going: I am still waiting.

  • I have just seen her in the corridor. — a moment ago
  • They have already eaten, so do not cook. — earlier than expected
  • Has the postman been yet? — question, end of the clause
  • We have not finished the report yet. — negative
Teaching sequence
1
Three markers

'just' = a moment ago; 'already' = sooner than expected (positive); 'yet' = up to now (questions/negatives, end position).

Support : 'I've just eaten. Have you finished yet?'

2
Position

just/already mid-position; yet at the end. Drill placement.

3
Production

Report task progress using all three.

Common pitfall : 'yet' goes at the END and only in questions/negatives ('not yet', 'Have you...yet?'); 'already' is for positives, usually mid-sentence.

Prepositions of time (in, on, at, for, since) A240 questions

Grammar note

at + clock times and the expressions at night, at the weekend, at Christmas; on + days and dates; in + months, seasons, years and parts of the day (in the morning). For duration, for + a length of time and since + the point at which it started.

  • The train leaves at 6.15 on Friday. — clock time, then day
  • She was born in March 1998, in the spring. — month, year, season
  • We have lived here for ten years. — length of time
  • I have not seen him since Easter. — starting point
Teaching sequence
1
Noticing in context

Hand out a short diary page describing one week — appointments, a birthday, a holiday, a train — with every time phrase in bold. Learners underline only the preposition in each phrase and sort the phrases into three piles headed AT, ON, IN, with a fourth pile for phrases that have no preposition at all. Give no rule before the sorting is finished.

Support : Support: one-page diary text “Kate’s week” with the fifteen time phrases in bold.

2
Guided discovery: precision ladder

Elicit from the sorted piles that the three prepositions form a ladder of precision: AT for a point (at 7 o’clock, at midnight, at the weekend in British English), ON for a whole day or date (on Monday, on 5 June, on Christmas Day), IN for anything longer (in June, in 2026, in the summer). Then test the ladder on the two traps: at night against in the morning, and on Friday morning, where the named day outranks the part of the day.

Support : Support: three-column wall chart AT / ON / IN, with a separate box for FOR / SINCE.

3
Controlled practice: for vs since

Drill the pair against the present perfect the class already knows. Say a date or a length of time and require a full sentence in reply: “2020” gives “I have lived here since 2020”, “three years” gives “I have lived here for three years”. Correct on the spot every “since three years” and make the learner say back which of the two is a length and which is a starting point.

4
Freer production: my timeline

Each learner draws a timeline of their own life and writes eight sentences about it, using at least one AT, one ON, one IN, one FOR, one SINCE and one AGO, plus one sentence with last or next and no preposition. Pairs exchange timelines and correct only the prepositions before the sentences are read aloud.

Common pitfall : Two errors dominate. Learners write “since three years” for a duration — insist on the test question: is it a length of time [for] or a point where the clock started [since]? And they add a preposition where English has none: “on last Monday”, “in next week”, “at every Sunday”. Teach next, last, this and every as a closed list that blocks the preposition, and correct it as a spelling-level automatism rather than by re-explaining the rule.

Prepositions of place & movement (in, on, at, to, into) A240 questions

Grammar note

For position: in = inside an area or volume, on = on a surface or a line, at = a point, an address or a place seen as an event. For movement: to gives the destination, into movement to the inside of something, and onto movement to a surface.

  • The milk is in the fridge. — inside a volume
  • Your ticket is on the table. — a surface
  • I will meet you at the entrance. — a point
  • She ran into the room, then drove to the airport. — into vs to
Teaching sequence
1
Noticing in context

Project a labelled picture of a flat and read out eight short statements about it: the keys are in the drawer, the poster is on the wall, somebody is waiting at the door. Learners point at the object each time and write only the preposition they hear, so that attention goes to the small word rather than to the vocabulary.

Support : Material: one A3 picture of a flat with numbered objects, plus a listening script of eight statements.

2
Guided discovery: in / on / at

Build a three-column poster on the board — in for an enclosed space or an area, on for a surface or a line, at for a point or an institution seen as a function — and have learners sort twenty cards into the columns: the box, the wall, the bus stop, London, the second floor, work, bed, the door. Then present the contrast pairs in the car versus on the bus, at school versus in the school, and in / on / at the corner, and make learners say what changes in the meaning, not which one is correct.

Support : Material: twenty word cards plus the three-column poster, kept on the wall for the whole unit.

3
Controlled practice: movement

Drill the movement set with mime: one learner acts out an instruction — walk into the room, get off the chair, go out of the door, walk across the classroom, go past the desk, climb onto the table — while the class calls out the preposition. Correct only the preposition, and insist on the two-word forms out of and in front of, which learners tend to shorten.

4
Freer production: directions

In pairs, learners describe the route from the school to their home in six sentences and then describe their own bedroom in six more, using at least ten different prepositions. Collect the errors on the board and correct them as a class, paying particular attention to any added preposition before home, abroad and downstairs.

Common pitfall : The commonest error by far is adding a preposition before an adverb of place: learners write “go to home”, “travel to abroad” or “go to downstairs” on the model of “go to work” and “go to school”. Treat home, abroad, upstairs, downstairs, outside and inside as a short closed list to be memorised as bare forms, and drill them in a rapid substitution chain — go to the office, go home, go to Spain, go abroad — so the contrast with real noun destinations is heard rather than explained.

Syntax & discourse

Question words + auxiliary A240 questions

Grammar note

A wh- question follows the order question word + auxiliary + subject + main verb: Where do you live? What has he done? With be as the main verb there is no extra auxiliary, only inversion: Where is she? The question word stands for the missing information.

  • When did the film start? — did + subject + base form
  • Why are you laughing? — be simply inverted
  • How long have they known each other? — have + subject + participle
  • Which bus should I take? — question word + noun
Teaching sequence
1
Consolidate wh- + aux

Drill how, why, how much/many, how long with the right auxiliary and tense. 'How long did you stay?'

2
Subject vs object Qs

Contrast 'Who saw you?' (subject, no do) with 'Who did you see?' (object, with do).

3
Production

Build an interview mixing subject and object questions.

Common pitfall : Subject questions need no auxiliary ('What happened?'), but object questions do ('What did you do?'). Learners often insert 'did' wrongly in subject questions.

Sequencers (first, then, after that, finally) A240 questions

Grammar note

Sequencers put the steps of a story or a process in order. A typical chain is first (of all), then, next, after that, and finally or in the end. They normally open the clause and are followed by a comma; the verb order after them does not change.

  • First, heat the oil in a large pan. — opening step
  • Then add the onions and stir. — next step
  • After that, pour in the tomatoes. — linking two stages
  • Finally, serve the sauce with pasta. — closing step
Teaching sequence
1
Order a narrative

Use first, then, next, after that, finally to sequence events. Arrange a scrambled story.

Support : 'First I woke up, then I had breakfast...'

2
before/after + -ing

Introduce 'before/after + -ing' for same-subject sequencing ('After eating, I left').

3
Production

Describe a daily routine or recipe with sequencers.

Common pitfall : 'after/before + -ing' needs the same subject as the main clause: 'After finishing, she left' (she finished and left).

Defining relative clauses (who, which, that) A240 questions

Grammar note

A defining relative clause tells you which person or thing is meant, so it takes no commas. Use who for people, which for things, that for either, and whose for possession. When the relative pronoun is the object of the clause, you can leave it out.

  • The woman who lives next door is a vet. — subject, person
  • This is the film (that) we saw last night. — object, can be omitted
  • A corkscrew is a tool which opens bottles. — thing
  • That is the boy whose bike was stolen. — possession
Teaching sequence
1
who/which/that

who for people, which for things, that for both. They identify which one. 'The man who lives here.'

Support : 'the book that I bought'

2
Subject vs object relative

Show object relatives where the pronoun can be omitted: 'the book (that) I bought'. Brief drill.

3
Production

Define people/objects in a guessing game using relative clauses.

Common pitfall : Don't add an extra pronoun: 'the man who lives here' (not 'who he lives here'); 'the book I read' (not 'the book I read it').

Question tags (isn't it? don't you?) A240 questions

Grammar note

A tag repeats the auxiliary of the sentence plus a pronoun subject: positive sentence, negative tag; negative sentence, positive tag. If there is no auxiliary, use do, does or did. Special forms: I am ... aren’t I?, Let’s ... shall we?, imperative ... will you?

  • You are coming, aren’t you? — positive sentence, negative tag
  • She has not phoned, has she? — negative sentence, positive tag
  • Tom likes jazz, doesn’t he? — no auxiliary, so does
  • Let’s take a taxi, shall we? — special form
Teaching sequence
1
Noticing in context

Read aloud a short conversation between two neighbours in which every second turn ends in a tag: “You're the new tenant, aren't you?”, “The lift isn't working, is it?”. Learners underline the two-word ending of each sentence and say, for each one, whether the speaker is checking something or genuinely asking.

Support : Support: an eight-line dialogue with the six tags printed in bold.

2
Guided discovery

Put four statements on the board, two positive and two negative, and elicit the tags. Mark each half with a plus or a minus so the class formulates the rule itself: a positive statement takes a negative tag, a negative statement takes a positive tag. Then add a second column for the auxiliary — copy the one already there, or bring in do, does or did when the statement has none.

Support : Support: a two-column grid, statement / tag, with eight rows to complete.

3
Controlled practice

Rapid oral drill: you read the statement, the class answers with the tag alone. Slot in the special cases every third item — I'm late…, Let's start…, Open the window…, There is a problem…, Nobody phoned… Correct two errors on the spot: [am I not] in place of [aren't I], and any negative tag after never, hardly, nobody or nothing.

4
Freer production with intonation

In pairs, learners guess three facts about their partner and check each one with a tag, using a falling voice when they are fairly sure and a rising voice when they are really asking. Correct polarity and auxiliary first; then ask each pair to report which of their tags were genuine questions and which were only seeking agreement.

Common pitfall : The commonest error by far is a negative tag after a sentence that is already negative without [not]: [He never calls, doesn't he?], [Nobody came, didn't they?]. Train learners to look for a hidden negative — never, hardly, rarely, seldom, nobody, nothing, no — and to use a positive tag whenever one is there.

Number & quantity

Quantifiers (much / many / a lot of) A240 questions

Grammar note

much goes with uncountable nouns and many with plural countable nouns, above all in questions and negatives. In positive statements English prefers a lot of or lots of, which suit both kinds of noun. The questions are How much...? and How many...?

  • How much water do you drink a day? — uncountable
  • I have not got many friends here. — plural, negative
  • She has a lot of homework this week. — positive, uncountable
  • There were lots of people at the concert. — positive, plural
Teaching sequence
1
Countable vs uncountable

'many' + plural, 'much' + uncountable; 'a lot of' for both. 'much/many' favour questions/negatives.

Support : 'How much time? How many people? a lot of money'

2
Positive → a lot of

Prefer 'a lot of' in positive statements ('I have a lot of work'); 'much/many' in questions/negatives.

3
Production

Talk about quantities (work, friends, money) choosing correctly.

Common pitfall : 'much' goes with uncountables ('much money') and 'many' with plurals ('many friends'); mixing them is a frequent A2 slip.

Ordinal numbers (first, second...) A240 questions

Grammar note

Ordinals give position in a series and are usually the number + -th: four → fourth, six → sixth. Irregular forms: first, second, third, fifth, eighth, ninth, twelfth; -y becomes -ieth (twenty → twentieth). In compounds only the last part changes, and the is normally used before them.

  • She lives on the ninth floor. — the + ordinal
  • It is my twenty-third birthday. — only the last element changes
  • Shakespeare wrote in the sixteenth century. — -teen + th
  • Short written forms: 1st, 2nd, 3rd, 4th, 20th.
Teaching sequence
1
Form & spelling

first, second, third (irregular), then -th (fourth, fifth...). Used for dates, floors, rankings.

Support : 'the third of May', 'the 21st floor'

2
Dates

BrE 'the fifth of June' / AmE 'June fifth'. Drill saying dates.

3
Production

Give birthdays and rank a top list with ordinals.

Common pitfall : first/second/third are irregular and 'fifth/twelfth' change spelling; ordinals (not cardinals) are used for dates and floors.

B1 B1 — Threshold

Communicative independence · simple arguments · structured narrative

Nouns & articles

Articles: a/the/zero (systematic review) B140 questions

Grammar note

Use a/an to introduce a singular countable noun for the first time or to classify it (she is a nurse). Use the once the noun is identified: mentioned before, unique, or defined by the context. Use no article with plural and uncountable nouns taken generally, and with most names, meals and languages.

  • I bought a jacket, and the jacket was half price. — new, then known
  • The sun was setting behind the hills. — unique thing
  • Cheese is made from milk. — general, uncountable
  • She studies French and has lunch at one. — language, meal
Teaching sequence
1
A decision routine

Build a flowchart: countable? specific/known? general plural or uncountable? → choose a/the/zero. Apply to a text.

Support : 'I bought a car. The car is red. Cars are useful.'

2
Fixed phrases

Note article-free expressions (go to bed, at school, by car) and 'the' with unique/known nouns.

3
Production

Correct a text where all articles have been removed.

Common pitfall : The hardest call is generic statements (zero article) vs specific ones ('the'): 'Tigers are dangerous' vs 'The tigers in this zoo'.

Compound nouns (bus stop, toothbrush) B140 questions

Grammar note

A compound noun is most often noun + noun: the first word says which kind, the second carries the meaning and the plural ending (a bus stop, two bus stops). The main stress falls on the first word. Spelling varies: open (bus stop), hyphenated (T-shirt) or one word (toothbrush).

  • There are three car parks in the town centre. — plural on the second noun
  • She left her toothbrush in the bathroom. — written as one word
  • Compare a race horse and a horse race. — the order changes the meaning
  • a SWIMMING pool — the stress is on the first word
Teaching sequence
1
Noun + noun

The first noun describes the second: 'a bus stop' = a stop for buses. Build and decode compounds.

Support : 'a coffee cup' (a cup for coffee)

2
First noun stays singular

The modifying noun is usually singular: 'a five-star hotel', 'a ten-minute walk'. Drill the pattern.

3
Production

Label objects and places using compound nouns.

Common pitfall : The first noun is normally singular even with a plural idea: 'a shoe shop' (sells shoes), not 'a shoes shop'.

Verbs & tenses

Present perfect continuous B140 questions

Grammar note

Form: have/has been + verb-ing (I have been waiting, she has been working). It links the past to now: an action that started earlier and is still going on, or one that has just stopped and explains the present situation. It stresses the activity and its duration, while the present perfect simple stresses the finished result.

  • She has been studying since nine o'clock. — still going on, since + start point
  • I've been running, that's why I'm out of breath. — recent activity, present result
  • How long have you been living here? — question form: how long + have + been + -ing
  • They've painted the kitchen. / They've been painting all day. — result vs activity
Teaching sequence
1
have been + -ing

Emphasise duration of a recent/ongoing activity: 'I've been studying for three hours.'

Support : 'She's been working all day.'

2
Activity vs result

Contrast with present perfect simple: continuous stresses the activity/duration; simple stresses the completed result.

3
Production

Explain why you look tired/busy using the continuous.

Common pitfall : Stative verbs avoid the continuous: 'I've known her for years' (not 'been knowing'). Use 'for' + duration, 'since' + start point.

Past perfect B140 questions

Grammar note

Form: had + past participle, the same for every person (I had gone, she had gone; negative hadn't gone). Use it for the earlier of two past events, when you are already talking about a past moment and need to step back before it. It is often introduced by after, before, when, because or by the time.

  • When we arrived, the film had already started. — earlier action before the past point
  • She had never flown before that trip. — experience up to a past moment
  • They were tired because they hadn't slept. — negative: hadn't + past participle
  • By the time he called, I had left. — by the time + past perfect
Teaching sequence
1
had + past participle

An action before another past action: 'When I arrived, they had already left.' Timeline work.

Support : 'She had finished before he came.'

2
Sequencing the past

Combine with past simple to show order. Drill 'because ... had ...' explanations.

3
Production

Narrate a past event explaining what had happened before.

Common pitfall : Use the past perfect only to step further back from an established past point — not as a general past tense. There must be a later past reference.

Future continuous B140 questions

Grammar note

Form: will be + verb-ing, unchanged for every person (I will be working, they'll be waiting; negative won't be working). Use it for an action in progress at a stated future moment, for something that will happen anyway as part of the normal course of events, and for polite enquiries about people's plans.

  • This time tomorrow I'll be flying to Rome. — in progress at a future moment
  • Don't call at eight, we'll be having dinner. — activity around that time
  • Will you be using the car tonight? — polite enquiry about plans
  • She won't be coming to the meeting. — negative: won't be + -ing
Teaching sequence
1
will be + -ing

An action in progress at a future time: 'This time tomorrow I'll be flying to Rome.'

Support : 'Don't call at 8 — we'll be having dinner.'

2
Polite enquiry

Also for polite questions about plans: 'Will you be using the car tonight?'

3
Production

Describe what you'll be doing at set times next week.

Common pitfall : Future continuous = in progress at a future moment, not the same as 'will' for a whole event: 'I'll be working' (during) vs 'I'll work' (the fact).

used to / would (past habits) B140 questions

Grammar note

Both take the base verb: used to live, would walk. Used to + base verb describes repeated past actions AND past states that are no longer true; would + base verb describes only repeated past actions, and normally needs a past time frame already set. Questions and negatives use did: did you use to, didn't use to.

  • I used to smoke, but I gave up ten years ago. — past habit, no longer true
  • We used to be neighbours. — past state, would is impossible here
  • Every summer my grandfather would take us fishing. — repeated action in a set past frame
  • Did you use to walk to school? — question: did + use to (no -d)
Teaching sequence
1
Past habits & states

'used to + base verb' for repeated past actions and past states no longer true: 'I used to live in Lagos.'

Support : 'We used to play outside.'

2
would for repeated actions

'would' can replace 'used to' for repeated past ACTIONS (not states): 'Every summer we would visit...'.

3
Production

Describe childhood habits with used to / would.

Common pitfall : 'would' works for repeated actions but NOT for past states: 'I used to have a dog' (not 'I would have a dog' for possession). Question/negative: 'did you use to...?'.

Passive voice (present & past) B140 questions

Grammar note

Form: the right tense of be + past participle. Present: am / is / are + participle (the room is cleaned every day). Past: was / were + participle (the bridge was built in 1890). The object of the active sentence becomes the subject; name the doer only if it matters, with by + agent.

  • English is spoken here. — present simple passive, doer unimportant
  • These photos were taken in Peru. — past simple passive, plural subject
  • The letters are not delivered on Sundays. — negative: are not + participle
  • Hamlet was written by Shakespeare. — by + agent when the doer matters
Teaching sequence
1
be + past participle

Focus on the action/result, not the doer: 'English is spoken here. The window was broken.'

Support : 'The cars are made in Japan.'

2
by + agent

Add 'by' only when the doer matters. Transform active → passive across present and past.

3
Production

Describe a process (how something is made) in the passive.

Common pitfall : Only verbs with an object can be made passive, and don't forget the right form of 'be': 'was built' (not 'was build'). Keep the participle, not the base verb.

First conditional (real future) B140 questions

Grammar note

Form: If + present simple, will + base verb (If it rains, we will stay in). The two clauses can swap order, and no comma is needed when the if-clause comes second. Use it for a future situation you consider realistically possible and its likely result. Will can be replaced by may, might, can, should or an imperative.

  • If you hurry, you'll catch the train. — real possibility, likely result
  • We'll go to the beach if the weather is fine. — if-clause second, no comma
  • If you see Tom, tell him to call me. — imperative in the main clause
  • If she doesn't leave now, she might miss it. — negative condition, might for less certainty
Teaching sequence
1
if + present, will + base

Real/likely future condition: 'If it rains, we'll stay home.' Note the present tense after 'if'.

Support : 'If you study, you'll pass.'

2
unless / when

Introduce 'unless' (= if not) and contrast 'if' (uncertain) with 'when' (certain). Drill paired clauses.

3
Production

Make promises/plans with first conditionals.

Common pitfall : No 'will' in the if-clause: 'If it rains' (not 'If it will rain'). The condition uses the present even though it refers to the future.

Second conditional (hypothetical) B140 questions

Grammar note

Form: If + past simple, would + base verb (If I had more time, I would learn Greek). The past tense here is not past time: it marks the situation as imaginary, unlikely or contrary to the present facts. With be, were is used for all persons in careful English, especially in the advice pattern If I were you.

  • If I won the lottery, I'd travel the world. — unlikely future situation
  • If he were taller, he'd play basketball. — were for all persons
  • If I were you, I'd apologise. — fixed pattern for advice
  • She would help you if she knew. / *if she would know — would stays in the main clause
Teaching sequence
1
if + past, would + base

Unreal/imaginary present or future: 'If I had money, I would travel.' The past form signals unreality.

Support : 'If I were you, I'd apologise.'

2
were for all persons

In the if-clause, 'were' is used for all persons in careful English ('If I were rich'). Contrast with first conditional.

3
Production

Discuss imaginary situations ('What would you do if...?').

Common pitfall : Don't put 'would' in the if-clause ('If I would have...' is wrong). Past tense after 'if', 'would' in the main clause.

Reported speech (statements) B140 questions

Grammar note

Report a statement with say or tell someone, plus optional that, and move the verb one step back in time: present simple goes to past simple, present continuous to past continuous, past simple and present perfect to past perfect, will to would, can to could. Pronouns and words of time and place change with the new speaker's viewpoint.

  • “I'm tired.” — She said (that) she was tired. — present continuous to past continuous
  • “We have finished.” — They told me they had finished. — tell + person, present perfect to past perfect
  • “I'll call you.” — He said he would call me. — will to would, you to me
  • “The Earth moves round the Sun.” — He said the Earth moves round the Sun. — no shift for a general truth
Teaching sequence
1
Backshift

Move tenses one step back after a past reporting verb: present→past, will→would, 'I'm tired' → 'He said he was tired.'

Support : 'I live here' becomes 'She said she lived there.'

2
Shift pronouns & time words

Adjust pronouns and references (now→then, tomorrow→the next day, here→there). Transformation drill.

3
Production

Report what classmates said in an earlier survey.

Common pitfall : Remember to shift pronouns and time/place words, not just the tense: 'I'll see you here tomorrow' becomes 'He said he would see me there the next day'.

Gerunds vs infinitives (verb patterns) B140 questions

Grammar note

After a verb, a second verb appears either as an -ing form or as to + base verb, and the first verb decides which. Enjoy, avoid, finish, mind, suggest, keep and practise take -ing; want, decide, hope, promise, offer, learn and agree take to + base. After all prepositions, only the -ing form is possible.

  • I enjoy cooking at the weekend. — enjoy + -ing
  • She decided to move abroad. — decide + to + base verb
  • He's good at drawing. — after a preposition, always -ing
  • I stopped smoking. / I stopped to smoke. — gave it up vs paused in order to
Teaching sequence
1
Verb-dependent

Some verbs take -ing (enjoy, finish, avoid), others take to-infinitive (want, decide, hope). Sort common verbs.

Support : 'enjoy reading' vs 'want to read'

2
Meaning change verbs

Note pairs that change meaning (stop doing vs stop to do; remember doing vs remember to do).

3
Production

Talk about likes/plans using the correct pattern after each verb.

Common pitfall : The choice is fixed by the first verb, not by a rule learners can guess: 'enjoy to read' is wrong — it must be 'enjoy reading'. Memorise verbs in patterns.

Past ability (could / was able to / managed to) B140 questions

Grammar note

Could + base verb states a general ability that lasted over a period of past time (she could swim at four). For one particular occasion when the action actually succeeded, use was/were able to or managed to. With verbs of perception and thinking (see, hear, feel, understand, remember) could is normal even for single moments.

  • My grandfather could speak four languages. — general ability over past time
  • The traffic was awful, but we managed to arrive on time. — one successful occasion
  • After hours of work she was able to open the file. — single achievement, effort implied
  • I could hear voices in the next room. — verb of perception, single moment
Teaching sequence
1
Noticing in context

Read out a short rescue story containing four ability forms: “She could swim before she could walk”, “The fire spread quickly, but everyone was able to escape”, “I couldn't open the emergency door” and “We could hear people shouting”. Ask learners to underline every way of talking about ability and to write beside each one whether it describes a skill over a period or one particular moment.

Support : Support: a ten-line printed narrative with the four ability forms in bold, plus a two-column grid headed “over a period / one moment”.

2
Guided discovery of the one-occasion rule

Put the two sentences “She could swim at four” and “He could escape from the burning house” side by side and ask the class which one sounds wrong and why. Lead them to state the rule themselves: could is fine for a general skill, but a single successful action needs was/were able to or managed to. Write the rule on the board in the learners' own words.

3
Controlled practice with the two exceptions

Drill twelve short prompts orally, mixing general skills, single successes, negatives and perception verbs. Insist that the negative loses the restriction, so “I couldn't open the door” is correct, and that see, hear, smell, feel, understand and remember keep could even at one precise moment.

Support : Support: a set of twelve prompt cards, each marked with a small icon for skill, one-off success, negative or perception.

4
Freer production: the difficult journey

In pairs, learners tell the story of a journey or a day when almost everything went wrong, using at least three ability forms and one managed to. Correct only two things at feedback stage: any could used for a single successful action, and the pattern after manage and succeed, which must be manage to do and succeed in doing.

Common pitfall : By far the commonest error is could for one successful action: learners write “The fire spread, but everyone could escape” instead of “was able to escape” or “managed to escape”. Reassure them that the negative is unrestricted — “couldn't escape” is perfectly correct — so the rule only has to be applied to affirmative single occasions.

Phrasal verbs (separable & inseparable) B140 questions

Grammar note

A phrasal verb is verb + particle, often with an idiomatic meaning. Separable ones allow either order with a noun object (turn off the light / turn the light off), but a pronoun object must go in the middle: turn it off. Inseparable ones keep verb and particle together: look after the baby, look after her.

  • Turn the radio off. / Turn off the radio. — noun object, either position
  • Turn it off. — pronoun object always in the middle
  • I ran into an old friend yesterday. — inseparable, meaning met by chance
  • We're looking forward to seeing you. — three-part verb, nothing can be inserted
Teaching sequence
1
Noticing in context

Read a short dialogue containing eight phrasal verbs: get up, turn off, look after, look forward to, break down, find out, put off, run into. Learners underline every verb + particle unit and answer one question only — could you guess the meaning from the two words alone? Do not give translations yet.

Support : Support: a twelve-line dialogue, target items in bold, plus a strip of the eight verbs cut up for sorting.

2
Guided discovery: four types

Learners sort the underlined verbs into four columns using two tests: does the verb take an object, and can a noun object stand after the particle? Elicit the four labels — intransitive, transitive separable, transitive inseparable, three-part — and have each pair add one more verb of their own to every column.

Support : Support: a four-column grid with turn off and look after already placed as models.

3
Controlled practice: pronoun drill

Drill the three-step transformation aloud: turn off the light → turn the light off → turn it off. Then contrast the inseparable pattern: look after the baby → look after her, with no middle step. Stop the class the moment anyone produces [turn off it] or [look her after] and rebuild the correct order on the board.

4
Freer production: register switch

In pairs, learners write a five-line informal message using six phrasal verbs, then rewrite it as a formal e-mail replacing each one with its Latinate equivalent — put off / postpone, find out / discover, give up / abandon, throw away / discard. Correct word order first, register second.

Common pitfall : The dominant error is putting a pronoun after the particle — [turn off it], [pick up them] — because learners meet the noun order [turn off the light] first and generalise it. Every time a separable verb is introduced, drill the pronoun version in the same breath as the noun version, and never let a separable verb be presented without one.

Reported questions, commands & requests B140 questions

Grammar note

In a reported question the word order becomes that of a statement, do/does/did disappears, and the sentence ends with a full stop: he asked where I lived. Yes/no questions need if or whether. Commands and requests use tell or ask + person + to + base verb, with not before to for the negative.

  • “Where do you work?” — She asked me where I worked. — statement order, no did
  • “Are you coming?” — He asked if I was coming. — yes/no question needs if or whether
  • “Sit down.” — The teacher told us to sit down. — tell + person + to + base verb
  • “Please don't be late.” — She asked me not to be late. — negative: not + to
Teaching sequence
1
Noticing the word-order shift

Put four pairs on the board, the direct question above and the reported version below: “Where do you live?” / He asked me where I lived. Ask learners to draw an arrow showing that the auxiliary do disappears and that the subject moves back in front of the verb, then to cross out the question mark themselves. Do not re-teach backshift here: treat it as known and keep the whole focus on order and punctuation.

Support : Support: handout of eight direct/reported pairs, one pair per line, wh- questions only, the auxiliary printed in grey so it can be struck through.

2
Guided discovery of if / whether

Give six yes/no questions with no question word and ask pairs to report them; they will feel that a link word is missing. Supply if and whether, then add two items containing “or not” and one after a preposition, such as “It depends on ___ he comes”, so that the class discovers for itself that only whether works in those two positions.

3
Controlled practice: verb + object + to

Call out orders and polite requests at speed — “Sit down!”, “Don't be late!”, “Could you open the window?” — and have the class answer chorally: He told me to sit down, He told me not to be late, She asked me to open the window. Correct two things only: the personal object after tell and ask, and the position of not immediately before to.

Support : Support: card set of twenty spoken prompts, ten orders and ten polite requests, plus a second set for advise, warn, remind, invite and beg.

4
Freer production: relaying a message

In threes, learner A reads a dialogue card containing two questions, one order and one request, and reports it orally to B, who writes the four reported sentences down; C checks the punctuation. Collect the written versions and correct only four things: word order in the questions, if/whether, the missing personal object after tell, and any stray question mark.

Common pitfall : Learners keep the interrogative form after the reporting verb — “He asked me where did I live”, “She asked was I coming” — and round it off with a question mark. Drill the reversal on its own before combining it with backshift: give the opening “He asked me where…” and let the class complete it, insisting that everything after the question word is ordered like a plain statement and that the sentence ends with a full stop.

Adjectives

Gradable vs non-gradable adjectives B140 questions

Grammar note

Gradable adjectives (big, cold, tired, interesting) describe a quality that can vary in degree, so they combine with very, quite, rather, a bit and take comparatives. Non-gradable adjectives are either extreme (enormous, freezing, exhausted, fascinating) or absolute (dead, perfect, digital); they take absolutely, completely or totally instead.

  • It was very cold last night. — gradable adjective with very
  • It was absolutely freezing. — extreme adjective with absolutely
  • The room was a bit dirty, in fact it was completely filthy. — gradable then extreme
  • That's totally impossible. — absolute adjective, no degrees
Teaching sequence
1
Two kinds

Gradable (big, hot, tired) take 'very/quite'; non-gradable/extreme (huge, freezing, exhausted) take 'absolutely/totally'.

Support : 'very hot' but 'absolutely freezing'

2
Matching intensifiers

Drill which intensifier fits which type. 'absolutely delicious', not 'very delicious' (in careful use).

3
Production

React to experiences choosing gradable/non-gradable adjectives + intensifier.

Common pitfall : Extreme adjectives already mean 'very', so 'very excellent' or 'very enormous' sounds wrong — use 'absolutely' instead.

-ed vs -ing adjectives (bored / boring) B140 questions

Grammar note

Both come from verbs of feeling. The -ing form describes the thing, person or situation that causes the feeling (the film was boring); the -ed form describes the person who experiences it (I was bored). The same pair works for interested/interesting, tired/tiring, excited/exciting, surprised/surprising, confused/confusing.

  • The journey was tiring, so we were tired. — cause then experiencer
  • I'm interested in history because history is interesting. — the pair in one sentence
  • Her reaction was surprising. — -ing describes the thing
  • The children were excited about the trip. — -ed describes the people who feel it
Teaching sequence
1
Feeling vs cause

-ed describes how you feel (bored, interested); -ing describes the thing causing it (boring, interesting).

Support : 'I'm bored. The film is boring.'

2
Common pairs

Drill excited/exciting, tired/tiring, surprised/surprising in matched sentences.

3
Production

Describe experiences and your reactions using both forms.

Common pitfall : Saying 'I am boring' means YOU bore others; 'I am bored' means you feel bored — a high-frequency, meaning-changing error.

Pronouns

Relative pronouns (whose, where, when) B140 questions

Grammar note

Use whose + noun for possession (his, her, their), where for a place and when for a time. The clause comes straight after the noun it describes: the girl whose father is a doctor, the café where we met, the year when she left. Whose works for people and for things.

  • The writer whose novel won the prize lives here. — possession
  • This is the hotel where we stayed last summer. — place, no extra preposition
  • I remember the day when we first met. — time
  • *the man which his car broke down → the man whose car broke down
Teaching sequence
1
Beyond who/which

'whose' = possession ('the man whose car...'), 'where' = place, 'when' = time. Extend defining clauses.

Support : 'the town where I grew up'

2
Choose the right pronoun

Match pronoun to function (owner/place/time). Combine sentence pairs.

3
Production

Describe people and places using whose/where/when.

Common pitfall : 'whose' shows possession (not 'who's' = who is): 'the writer whose book...'. 'where' replaces 'in which' for places, so no extra preposition is needed.

Reciprocal pronouns (each other) B140 questions

Grammar note

Each other and one another show a mutual action: two or more people each do the same thing to the other. They stand in object position, after the verb or after a preposition, and take an apostrophe for possession: each other’s.

  • Anna and Ben write to each other every week. — after a preposition
  • The two teams respect one another. — more formal alternative
  • They put on each other’s coats by mistake. — possessive form
  • We helped each other with the homework. — direct object
Teaching sequence
1
Mutual action

'each other / one another' for a two-way action: 'They love each other.' Contrast with reflexive (themselves).

Support : 'We help each other.'

2
With prepositions

'talk to each other, look at one another'. Brief drill on placement.

3
Production

Describe a relationship/team using 'each other'.

Common pitfall : 'each other' (mutual) is not 'themselves' (each to him/herself): 'They looked at each other' vs 'They looked at themselves in the mirror'.

Adverbs & negation

Adverbs of degree (quite, really, a bit) B140 questions

Grammar note

Degree adverbs go before the adjective or adverb they modify and set its strength: a bit and slightly, then quite, fairly and pretty, then very and really, then absolutely and completely. Before a main verb they take mid position: I really enjoyed it.

  • The film was quite good, but a bit long. — mid scale, then a small amount
  • She speaks really clearly. — degree adverb before another adverb
  • It was absolutely freezing. — with a non-gradable adjective
  • I quite like jazz. — mid position, before the verb
Teaching sequence
1
Strengthen or soften

Scale modifiers: a bit / quite / really / extremely before adjectives. 'It's quite cold / really cold.'

Support : 'a bit tired', 'really happy'

2
Register & nuance

Note BrE 'quite' can mean 'fairly' (quite good) or 'completely' (quite right) by adjective type. Brief awareness.

3
Production

Give graded opinions on films/food using degree adverbs.

Common pitfall : With extreme adjectives, 'a bit/very' don't fit ('a bit enormous' is odd). Match the modifier to gradable vs non-gradable adjectives.

Comparative & superlative adverbs B140 questions

Grammar note

Short adverbs with the same form as the adjective add -er and -est: fast, hard, late, early (earlier, earliest). Adverbs ending in -ly take more and most: more carefully, most quickly. Irregulars: well, better, best; badly, worse, worst; far, further, furthest. Use than after a comparative.

  • He drives more carefully than his brother. — -ly adverb with more
  • She got up earliest of all. — short adverb, superlative
  • They played worse than last week. — irregular form of badly
  • Could you speak a little more slowly, please? — softened comparative
Teaching sequence
1
more / most + adverb

Most adverbs use more/most: 'more carefully, the most quickly'. Short ones add -er/-est (faster, hardest).

Support : 'He drives more slowly than me.'

2
Irregulars

well→better→best, badly→worse→worst, far→further. Drill alongside adjective comparison.

3
Production

Compare how people do things ('She works harder than...').

Common pitfall : Compare actions with adverbs, not adjectives: 'He runs faster' / 'She sings more beautifully' (not 'more beautiful' for the action).

Linking adverbs (however, therefore) B140 questions

Grammar note

Linking adverbs such as however, therefore, moreover, nevertheless, otherwise and instead join ideas between sentences rather than inside one clause. They usually open the second sentence and are followed by a comma, but they can also sit in mid position between commas, or at the end.

  • It was raining. However, we went out. — contrast, front position
  • The train was cancelled; therefore, we drove. — result after a semicolon
  • The plan is expensive. Moreover, it is slow. — adding a further point
  • We were tired. We finished the job, nevertheless. — end position
Teaching sequence
1
Logical links in writing

however (contrast), therefore/so (result), in addition (adding), for example. Position at clause start with a comma.

Support : 'It rained; however, we went out.'

2
Punctuation

Show semicolon/full stop before, comma after. Contrast with conjunctions (but/so) that join within one sentence.

3
Production

Improve a choppy paragraph by adding linking adverbs.

Common pitfall : Linking adverbs are not conjunctions: punctuate with a full stop or semicolon, then a comma ('..., it rained. However, ...'), not a comma alone joining two clauses.

Syntax & discourse

Non-defining relative clauses B140 questions

Grammar note

A non-defining clause adds extra, non-essential information about a noun that is already identified. It is marked off by commas and introduced by who, whom, which, whose or where, and the relative pronoun is always kept. Which can also refer back to a whole clause.

  • My brother, who lives in Leeds, is a nurse. — extra detail about a known person
  • Oxford, where she studied, is very beautiful. — place
  • He passed the exam, which surprised everyone. — which refers to the whole idea
  • Our neighbour, whose dog barks all night, apologised. — possession
Teaching sequence
1
Extra information

Add non-essential detail between commas: 'My brother, who lives in Paris, is a doctor.' The clause can be removed.

Support : 'The book, which I loved, is out of print.'

2
No 'that', no omission

Use who/which (not that) and never omit the pronoun. Contrast with defining clauses.

3
Production

Add extra information about known people/things in commas.

Common pitfall : Non-defining clauses need commas and forbid 'that' ('My car, which is red...', not 'that is red'); the relative pronoun can't be dropped.

Time clauses (when, while, as soon as) B140 questions

Grammar note

After when, while, as soon as, until, before, after and by the time, a present tense carries future meaning, because the main clause already shows the future. Use the present perfect to stress that one action finishes before the other. If the time clause comes first, put a comma after it.

  • I will call you as soon as I arrive. — present simple for the future
  • When you have finished, we can eat. — present perfect: one action first
  • While she was cooking, the phone rang. — background action in the past
  • By the time we get there, it will be dark. — comma after a fronted clause
Teaching sequence
1
Present for future

After when, as soon as, until, before, after — use the present (not 'will') for future time: 'I'll call when I arrive.'

Support : 'Wait until he comes.'

2
while + continuous

'while' often pairs with continuous for background ('while I was cooking'). Drill clause combinations.

3
Production

Plan a sequence of future actions with time clauses.

Common pitfall : No 'will' after time conjunctions referring to the future: 'when I get there' (not 'when I will get there'). The present carries the future meaning.

Reason & result (because, so, since) B140 questions

Grammar note

Because, since and as introduce a clause giving the reason; because of, due to and owing to are followed by a noun phrase. So introduces the result, and so or such … that expresses result with intensity. Since and as often open the sentence; because typically answers a Why question.

  • We stayed in because it was raining. — reason clause
  • The match was cancelled because of the snow. — reason with a noun
  • It was late, so we took a taxi. — result clause
  • It was such a good film that we watched it twice. — intensity and result
Teaching sequence
1
Cause vs consequence

because/since/as introduce a reason; so introduces a result. 'I stayed in because it rained / It rained, so I stayed in.'

Support : 'Since it's late, let's go.'

2
Register & position

'since/as' often start the sentence for known reasons; 'because' usually follows. Brief contrast.

3
Production

Explain decisions giving reason and result.

Common pitfall : 'because' + clause (because it was late) vs 'because of' + noun (because of the rain): don't mix the two structures.

Purpose clauses (to / in order to / so that) B140 questions

Grammar note

Purpose is normally expressed with to + infinitive, or with the more formal in order to and so as to; the negatives are in order not to and so as not to. Use so that + subject + modal (can, will, could, would) when the person performing the second action is different.

  • She went into town to buy a coat. — simple to-infinitive
  • He left early so as not to miss the train. — negative purpose
  • I spoke slowly so that everyone could understand. — a different subject
  • In order to apply, you must register first. — formal, front position
Teaching sequence
1
Same subject → to

Same subject uses 'to / in order to + base verb': 'I came here to learn English.'

Support : 'She left early to catch the train.'

2
Different subject → so that

Different subjects need 'so that + clause': 'I spoke slowly so that everyone could understand.'

3
Production

State goals, switching between 'to' and 'so that'.

Common pitfall : Don't use 'for + -ing' to express a person's purpose: 'I came to learn' (not 'for learning'). 'for + -ing' describes a thing's function, not someone's aim.

Number & quantity

few / a few / little / a little B140 questions

Grammar note

A few and few go with countable plural nouns; a little and little go with uncountable nouns. With a, the meaning is positive: some, enough. Without a, the meaning is negative: hardly any. Very few and very little sharpen the negative sense; only a few and only a little sound limiting.

  • We still have a few eggs left. — countable, positive
  • There is a little milk in the jug. — uncountable, positive
  • Few people came to the meeting. — countable, hardly any
