  • He has very little patience. — uncountable, hardly any
Teaching sequence
1
Countable vs uncountable

a few/few + plural; a little/little + uncountable. 'a few friends', 'a little time'.

Support : 'a few books', 'a little water'

2
With vs without 'a'

'a few/a little' = some (positive nuance); 'few/little' = almost none (negative nuance). Contrast pairs.

3
Production

Describe resources you have using the four forms with the right nuance.

Common pitfall : 'a few' (some) vs 'few' (hardly any) flips the meaning: 'I have a few friends' (positive) vs 'I have few friends' (lonely).

both / either / neither B140 questions

Grammar note

Both means the two together and takes a plural verb. Either means one or the other and neither means not one and not the other; both take a singular verb. Use both, either or neither of + the, my or these + plural noun, and the pairs both … and, either … or, neither … nor.

  • Both my sisters live abroad. — the two together, plural verb
  • Either day suits me. — one or the other, singular
  • Neither of the answers is correct. — negative meaning, singular verb
  • She speaks neither French nor German. — paired form
Teaching sequence
1
Two-item quantifiers

both = the two together; either = one or the other; neither = not one and not the other.

Support : 'Both are good. / Neither works.'

2
Agreement & structure

'both + plural verb'; 'neither/either + singular verb'. 'both...and', 'either...or', 'neither...nor'. Drill pairs.

3
Production

Compare two options using both/either/neither.

Common pitfall : 'neither' is already negative, so the verb stays positive and singular: 'Neither of them is here' (not 'aren't').

B2 B2 — Upper-Intermediate

Full autonomy · arguing · nuancing · register

Nouns & articles

Abstract & collective nouns (agreement) B240 questions

Grammar note

Abstract nouns such as information, advice and progress are uncountable: singular verb, no plural -s, no a or an. Collective nouns (team, government, family) take a singular verb when the group acts as one unit and, in British English, a plural verb when the members act individually.

  • The information was very useful. — uncountable abstract noun
  • The committee has reached a decision. — the group as one unit
  • The team are arguing among themselves. — members acting separately, BrE
  • The police have arrested two men. — noun that is always plural
Teaching sequence
1
Singular or plural verb?

Collective nouns (team, government, family) take a singular OR plural verb in BrE depending on whether the group acts as one body or as individuals.

Support : 'The team is strong' vs 'The team are arguing'

2
BrE vs AmE

Note AmE prefers the singular ('The team is...'). Drill choosing the verb form by meaning and variety.

3
Production

Write sentences about organisations, choosing agreement to match the intended sense.

Common pitfall : Some collectives are always plural ('the police are', 'people are'); be consistent within a sentence — don't switch from 'is' to 'their' for the same noun.

Article nuances (institutions, generalisations) B240 questions

Grammar note

Drop the article with institutions used for their purpose: at school, in hospital, in prison, at university, in bed. Use the when you mean the building or a specific one: at the school. Generalisations take a plural or uncountable noun with no article, while the + singular names a whole class in formal style.

  • Her son is at university in Bristol. — institution, its purpose
  • I parked at the university to meet her. — the place itself
  • Happiness is hard to define. — uncountable, general, no article
  • The smartphone has changed daily life. — the + singular for a whole class
Teaching sequence
1
Institution vs building

Zero article for the institutional purpose (go to hospital/school/prison) vs 'the' for the physical place (go to the hospital to visit).

Support : 'in hospital' (as a patient) vs 'at the hospital' (the building)

2
Generic 'the'

Contrast generic plural (zero) with generic 'the' + singular for species/inventions ('The tiger is endangered'). Brief drill.

3
Production

Edit a text fixing institution vs building and generic uses.

Common pitfall : BrE drops 'the' for the institutional sense ('She's at university'); AmE often keeps it. The choice signals purpose vs place.

Verbs & tenses

Future perfect B240 questions

Grammar note

Form: will have + past participle. It looks forward to a point in the future and says the action will already be over by then, typically with by, by the time, before or in two weeks. For a duration lasting up to that point, use will have been + -ing.

  • By Friday I will have finished the report. — completed before a future point
  • She won’t have arrived before six. — negative form
  • Will you have eaten by then? — question form
  • In June we will have been living here for ten years. — duration
Teaching sequence
1
will have + past participle

An action completed before a point in the future: 'By next year, I'll have graduated.'

Support : 'By 6 p.m. they will have arrived.'

2
With 'by' / 'by the time'

Pair with 'by' + time or 'by the time + present'. Contrast with future simple/continuous on a timeline.

3
Production

Set goals: what you'll have achieved by certain future dates.

Common pitfall : After 'by the time' use the present, not a future: 'By the time you arrive, I'll have finished' (not 'will arrive').

Third conditional (past unreal) B240 questions

Grammar note

If + past perfect, then would / could / might have + past participle. It imagines a past that did not happen and its unreal result, so the facts are always the opposite of the sentence. In formal writing, if can be dropped and had inverted: Had I known…

  • If I had studied, I would have passed. — I did not study, so I failed.
  • She wouldn’t have missed the train if she had left earlier. — result clause first, no comma
  • If they had asked me, I could have helped. — could / might have for a possible result
  • Had we booked sooner, the seats would have been cheaper. — inversion, no if
Teaching sequence
1
if + past perfect, would have + pp

Imagining a different past: 'If I had studied, I would have passed.' Both clauses look back and are unreal.

Support : 'If she had left earlier, she wouldn't have missed it.'

2
Contracted forms

Drill the heavily contracted spoken forms ('If I'd known, I'd have called'). Listening practice.

3
Production

Express regrets about past decisions in the third conditional.

Common pitfall : Keep 'would have' OUT of the if-clause: 'If I had known' (not 'If I would have known' — a very common error).

Mixed conditionals B240 questions

Grammar note

Each half keeps the form of its own time. Past cause with a present result: If + past perfect … would + base verb (now). Present or permanent cause with a past result: If + past simple … would have + past participle. Time markers such as now or yesterday show which mix you need.

  • If I had taken that job, I would be living in Berlin now. — past cause, present result
  • If she weren’t so shy, she would have spoken up at the meeting. — lasting trait, past result
  • If they hadn’t missed the flight, they would be here with us.
  • If I spoke Japanese, I would have understood the film last night.
Teaching sequence
1
Cross time references

Mix a past condition with a present result, or vice versa: 'If I had saved money (past), I would be rich now (present).'

Support : 'If I were taller, I would have made the team.'

2
Identify the time of each clause

Analyse which clause is past and which present, then match the right forms. Sorting drill.

3
Production

Link a past cause to a present consequence about your own life.

Common pitfall : Each clause keeps the form for its OWN time: past perfect for the past part, would + base for the present result — don't align them to one tense.

Passive (all tenses; passive reporting) B240 questions

Grammar note

Passive = be, carrying the tense of the active verb, + past participle; the agent follows by only when it adds information. Reporting structures: It is said / thought / believed that + clause, or subject + is said + to + infinitive, with to have + participle for an earlier time.

  • The bridge was built in 1890. — past simple passive
  • The road is being resurfaced this week. — continuous passive
  • All the files have been deleted. — present perfect passive
  • He is believed to have left the country. — passive reporting, earlier time
Teaching sequence
1
Extend across tenses

Build the passive in perfect, continuous and modal forms: 'has been done, is being built, must be finished.'

Support : 'The road is being repaired.'

2
Reporting passive

Impersonal reporting: 'It is said that... / He is believed to be...'. Useful for objective/academic register.

3
Production

Rewrite a news item using passive and reporting structures.

Common pitfall : In continuous passives keep 'being' ('is being built', not 'is built' for the ongoing process); in 'It is said that...' the 'it' is obligatory.

Modals of deduction (must/might/can't have) B240 questions

Grammar note

must / may / might / could / can’t + have + past participle judges how likely a past event was. must have = you are sure it happened; can’t have or couldn’t have = you are sure it did not; may / might / could have = it is one possibility; may not / might not have = possibly not.

  • The ground is wet — it must have rained in the night.
  • She might have forgotten the meeting. — one possibility among several
  • They can’t have arrived yet; the flight lands at six.
  • He may not have seen your message. — negative possibility, not certainty
Teaching sequence
1
Certainty scale (present)

must (sure it's true), might/could/may (possible), can't (sure it's false): 'He must be tired. / It can't be true.'

Support : 'She might be at home.'

2
Past deduction

modal + have + past participle: 'must have left, might have forgotten, can't have seen.' Drill present vs past.

3
Production

Speculate about a mystery picture/situation, present and past.

Common pitfall : For 'impossible', use 'can't have' not 'mustn't have': 'He can't have done it' (logical impossibility), not 'mustn't have done'.

Causative (have/get something done) B240 questions

Grammar note

have or get + object + past participle says that you arrange for somebody else to do the job. All the tense and modal changes fall on have or get: I have my hair cut, I had it cut, I am getting it cut, I must get it cut. get is more informal; add by + agent only when the doer matters.

  • We’re having the kitchen painted next week. — a decorator does the work
  • I got my laptop repaired yesterday. — informal get
  • She has had her passport stolen. — misfortune, not something arranged
  • You should get your eyes tested by a specialist. — agent added with by
Teaching sequence
1
have + object + past participle

Someone else does the job for you: 'I had my car repaired. / I'm getting my hair cut.'

Support : 'We had the house painted.'

2
have vs get; misfortune

'get' is more informal; also for unwanted events ('She had her bag stolen'). Drill the word order.

3
Production

Describe services you arrange using the causative.

Common pitfall : Word order is object + past participle: 'I had my car repaired' (not 'I had repaired my car', which means you did it yourself).

'wish' / 'if only' B240 questions

Grammar note

wish and if only take a step back in tense: past simple for a present situation you want to be different, past perfect for a regret about the past, and would + base verb to complain about somebody else’s habit or an irritating situation. were is possible for every person after wish.

  • I wish I had more free time. — present situation, untrue now
  • If only I had listened to her! — regret about the past
  • I wish you would stop interrupting me. — complaint about someone else
  • She wishes she were taller. — were with a singular subject
Teaching sequence
1
Three patterns

wish + past (present regret: 'I wish I had a car'); wish + past perfect (past regret: 'I wish I had studied'); wish + would (annoyance: 'I wish you would listen').

Support : 'If only I were taller.'

2
Map wish to conditionals

Link the backshift logic to second/third conditionals. Sorting by time and meaning.

3
Production

Express present and past regrets and complaints with wish/if only.

Common pitfall : 'wish + would' can't refer to yourself (you control your own actions): 'I wish I could...' not 'I wish I would...'.

Adjectives

Adjectives as nouns (the rich, the unemployed) B240 questions

Grammar note

the + adjective, with no noun after it and no plural -s, names a whole class of people: the rich, the poor, the young, the elderly, the homeless, the unemployed, the injured. The same pattern covers nationality adjectives ending in -sh, -ch or -ese: the British, the French, the Chinese.

  • The rich get richer. — plural verb, no -s on the adjective
  • This charity works with the homeless and the elderly.
  • The Spanish are proud of their cooking. — nationality adjective
  • *the unemployeds → the unemployed
Teaching sequence
1
the + adjective = group

'the + adjective' names a whole group of people, plural in sense: 'the rich, the elderly, the homeless.'

Support : 'The young often move to cities.'

2
Plural agreement

These take a plural verb and no -s: 'The unemployed are...'. Contrast with 'a rich person'.

3
Production

Write social-issue sentences using adjective-as-noun groups.

Common pitfall : 'the rich' means rich people in general and takes a plural verb; it can't refer to one person ('a rich man', not 'a rich').

Order & modification of adjectives (intensifiers) B240 questions

Grammar note

Before a noun, adjectives line up as opinion, size, age, shape, colour, origin, material, purpose: a lovely small old round brown Italian leather bag. Gradable adjectives take very, quite, rather, extremely; ungradable ones (extreme or absolute) take absolutely, completely, utterly, totally.

  • a beautiful large wooden table — opinion, size, material
  • two small black plastic chairs — number, size, colour, material
  • It was absolutely freezing. — ungradable: not very freezing
  • The film was quite interesting but rather long. — gradable adjectives
Teaching sequence
1
Fine-tune intensity

Layer intensifiers/downtoners: 'a remarkably well-designed, surprisingly cheap phone.' Match intensifier to adjective type.

Support : 'utterly convincing', 'fairly reliable'

2
Hyphenated pre-modifiers

Compound modifiers before nouns are hyphenated ('a well-known author', 'a five-year-old child'). Drill.

3
Production

Write a product review with precisely modified adjectives.

Common pitfall : Hyphenate compound modifiers before the noun ('a well-known writer') but not after ('the writer is well known').

Pronouns

Indefinite pronouns (someone, anything, no one) B240 questions

Grammar note

Build them from some-, any-, no- or every- plus -one and -body for people, -thing for things, -where for places. some- goes in positive statements and in offers or requests, any- in questions and after a negative verb or with the sense of it doesn’t matter which, no- with a positive verb, every- for the whole set.

  • Someone left a bag by the door. — positive statement
  • Would you like something to drink? — offer, so some-
  • Did you notice anything unusual? — question
  • Sit anywhere you like. — any- meaning it doesn’t matter which
Teaching sequence
1
some-/any-/no-/every- families

Build the grid (someone/anyone/no one/everyone; something/anything...). some- in positives, any- in Q/neg.

Support : 'Is anyone there? Nobody answered.'

2
Singular agreement & they

They take a singular verb ('Everyone is here') but singular 'they' for reference ('Someone left their bag').

3
Production

Describe a scene/situation using indefinite pronouns.

Common pitfall : 'nobody/no one' is already negative — don't add another negative ('I didn't see anybody', not 'didn't see nobody'). The verb is singular: 'Everybody knows'.

'it' & 'there' as dummy subjects B240 questions

Grammar note

there + be introduces the existence of something new, and be agrees with the noun that follows: There is a problem, There are two problems, There was, There will be. it fills the empty subject slot for weather, time, dates, distance and temperature, and before an adjective + to-infinitive or that-clause.

  • There are three messages for you. — plural noun after there
  • It’s half past four and it’s getting dark. — time and weather
  • It’s a long way to the station. — distance
  • It is important to arrive on time. — it anticipates the to-infinitive
Teaching sequence
1
Two empty subjects

'there' introduces existence ('There is a problem'); 'it' fills the subject slot for weather, time, distance and to delay a clause ('It is hard to say').

Support : 'It's raining. It's important to rest.'

2
Anticipatory 'it'

'It' postpones a long subject: 'It is surprising that he left' (= That he left is surprising). Transformation drill.

3
Production

Comment on situations using anticipatory 'it' and existential 'there'.

Common pitfall : English needs a grammatical subject, so don't drop it: 'It is raining' / 'There is a man' — never 'Is raining' or 'Is a man'.

Adverbs & negation

Adverb position & focus adverbs (only, even) B240 questions

Grammar note

Adverbs of frequency and degree stand before the main verb, but after be and after the first auxiliary. Adverbs of manner, place and time go at the end, in that order. Focus adverbs such as only, even, also and just come immediately before the word or phrase they pick out.

  • I have never seen that film. — after the first auxiliary
  • She is always cheerful in the morning. — after be
  • He played brilliantly at the club last night. — manner, place, time
  • Even the teacher laughed. — focus on the subject
Teaching sequence
1
Position changes meaning

Focus adverbs (only, even, just) attach to what follows: 'Only I saw her' vs 'I only saw her' vs 'I saw only her.' Compare meanings.

Support : 'She even cleaned the windows.'

2
Place next to the focus

Put the adverb immediately before the word it limits. Drill by shifting 'only' through a sentence.

3
Production

Write sentences where moving 'only' changes the meaning, and gloss each.

Common pitfall : Misplacing 'only' shifts the meaning: 'I only love you' (nothing but love) vs 'I love only you' (no one else). Position it next to its focus.

Negative & limiting adverbs (hardly, rarely) B240 questions

Grammar note

hardly, scarcely, barely, seldom, rarely, never and little already carry the negative, so the verb after them stays positive. Put one of them at the front of the sentence for emphasis and the subject and auxiliary invert. Hardly and no sooner are completed by when and than.

  • She seldom speaks in meetings. — positive verb, negative meaning
  • Hardly anyone turned up. — almost nobody
  • Never have I heard such nonsense. — front position forces inversion
  • No sooner had we sat down than the phone rang.
Teaching sequence
1
Near-negative meaning

hardly/scarcely/barely (= almost not), rarely/seldom (= almost never). The clause is positive in form.

Support : 'I can hardly hear you.'

2
No double negative

Because they are already negative in sense, don't add 'not': 'I hardly ever go' (not 'don't hardly').

3
Production

Describe infrequent habits using rarely/seldom/hardly ever.

Common pitfall : 'hardly' is near-negative, so 'I can't hardly' is a double negative; and 'hardly' (almost not) is unrelated to 'hard' (with effort).

Syntax & discourse

Concession (although, despite, in spite of) B240 questions

Grammar note

These link two ideas that clash. although, though and even though are followed by a subject + verb; despite and in spite of by a noun, an -ing form, or the fact that + clause; however, nevertheless and even so open a new sentence after a full stop or semicolon and take a comma.

  • Although it was raining, we walked home. — subject + verb after although
  • In spite of the noise, she slept well. — noun phrase
  • He passed despite having studied very little. — -ing form
  • The plan is risky. Nevertheless, we will try it. — new sentence, comma
Teaching sequence
1
Clause vs noun

although/though/even though + clause; despite/in spite of + noun or -ing. 'Although it rained...' / 'Despite the rain...'.

Support : 'In spite of being tired, she continued.'

2
Transform between them

Rewrite 'although + clause' as 'despite + noun/-ing' and back. Drill the structural switch.

3
Production

Concede a point in an argument using all three forms.

Common pitfall : 'despite/in spite of' take a noun or -ing, never a clause: 'despite the delay' or 'despite being late' (not 'despite it was late').

Cause & result connectors (consequently, due to) B240 questions

Grammar note

Cause has two patterns: a full clause after because, since and as, or a noun phrase after because of, due to and owing to. For result, so joins two clauses inside one sentence, while therefore, consequently, as a result and thus normally open a new sentence and are followed by a comma.

  • Since the lift was broken, we took the stairs. — cause clause
  • The match was cancelled because of the ice. — noun phrase
  • Sales fell sharply; consequently, two shops closed.
  • The road was blocked, so we turned back. — so joins two clauses
Teaching sequence
1
Formal links

due to/owing to + noun (cause); consequently/as a result/therefore (result, linking adverbs). Build a formal register set.

Support : 'Sales fell; consequently, prices rose.'

2
due to vs because of

'due to' traditionally follows 'be' ('The delay was due to fog'); 'because of' modifies a verb. Brief usage note.

3
Production

Write a cause-effect paragraph in formal register.

Common pitfall : 'due to/owing to' need a noun ('due to the strike'), while 'because' needs a clause ('because there was a strike') — keep the structures separate.

Cleft sentences (It was... / What...) B240 questions

Grammar note

A cleft splits one clause into two to spotlight a single element. It-cleft: it + be + focus + who/that + the rest (It was Anna who paid). Wh-cleft: what + clause + be + focus (What I need is time). The verb be agrees with it or what, and the focused part carries the new information.

  • It was Anna who paid the bill. — focus on the person
  • It was on Friday that we met, not on Saturday. — focus on the time
  • What I need is a decent night’s sleep. — focus placed at the end
  • What she did was rewrite the whole report. — focus on the action
Teaching sequence
1
Two cleft types

It-cleft highlights one element ('It was John who called'); wh-cleft fronts a clause ('What I need is time'). Mark the focus.

Support : 'It was in 2010 that we met.'

2
Build from a neutral sentence

Take 'John broke the window' and produce both clefts emphasising different parts. Transformation drill.

3
Production

Re-emphasise facts in a short text using clefts.

Common pitfall : Match the relative word to the focus in it-clefts: 'It was John who called' (person) vs 'It was the noise that woke me' (thing).

Participle clauses B240 questions

Grammar note

A participle clause has no subject and no tense of its own: it borrows both from the main clause. Use the -ing form for active meaning (Feeling tired, she left), the past participle for passive meaning (Built in 1890, the house needs work), and having + past participle for an action completed earlier.

  • Feeling tired, she went to bed early. — reason, active -ing form
  • Built in 1890, the house needs rewiring. — passive, past participle
  • Having handed in his essay, he finally relaxed. — earlier action
  • Anyone wanting a ticket should queue here. — reduced relative clause
Teaching sequence
1
Reduce a clause

Shorten a clause to a participle: 'Feeling tired, she left' (= Because she felt tired). Present (-ing) for active, past (-ed) for passive.

Support : 'Built in 1900, the house is protected.'

2
Same-subject rule

The participle's implied subject must match the main clause. Combine sentence pairs.

3
Production

Condense a descriptive paragraph using participle clauses.

Common pitfall : Avoid the dangling participle: the implied subject must be the main subject — 'Walking home, I saw...' (not 'Walking home, the rain started').

Number & quantity

Quantifiers review (all/most/none of) B240 questions

Grammar note

Use all, most, some, few, none with a bare noun for general statements (Most students work hard). Before a determiner or a pronoun you must add of: most of the students, none of them, all of us. With all and both, of is optional before the. None of takes a singular or a plural verb.

  • Most people enjoy a holiday. — general statement, no of
  • Most of the people in my team are new. — specific, defined group
  • None of us knew the answer. — of is obligatory before a pronoun
  • All (of) the tickets had sold out. — of optional before the
Teaching sequence
1
With or without 'of'

General: 'most people' (no of); specific: 'most of the people' (+ of + the/possessive/pronoun). Contrast the two.

Support : 'All children / all of the children in this class'

2
Agreement with 'none of'

'none of' + plural can take singular (formal) or plural (informal) verb. Drill the structures.

3
Production

Report survey results using all/most/some/none of.

Common pitfall : Drop 'of' for general statements ('most people are kind'); add 'of the' only for a specific, defined group ('most of the people here').

C1 C1 — Advanced

Extended mastery · style · implicature · complexity

Nouns & syntax

Complex noun phrases (pre/post-modification) C140 questions

Grammar note

A noun phrase is built round a head noun. Premodifiers precede it in a fixed order: determiner, adverb plus adjective, then noun modifier (a badly designed safety system). Postmodifiers follow it: prepositional phrases, relative and participle clauses, to-infinitives. Only postmodification can be expanded indefinitely.

  • a surprisingly effective new marketing strategy — premodification only
  • the report on air quality that we published last year — two postmodifiers
  • the decision to close the factory — to-infinitive postmodifier
  • the students taking the exam in June — participle clause after the head
Teaching sequence
1
Pack the noun phrase

Combine pre-modifiers (adjectives, nouns) and post-modifiers (prepositional phrases, relative and participle clauses) around one head noun.

Support : 'the recently published report on climate change'

2
Compress for headlines/academia

Condense a full sentence into a dense noun phrase. Practise on news and academic style.

3
Production

Turn three short sentences into a single rich noun phrase.

Common pitfall : Heavy stacking creates attachment ambiguity (what does the final phrase modify?). Reread to ensure each modifier clearly attaches to its head.

Nominalisation (academic style) C140 questions

Grammar note

Nominalisation turns a verb or an adjective into a noun (reduce → reduction, able → ability) so that a whole clause becomes a noun phrase: prices rose sharply → the sharp rise in prices. Adverbs become adjectives, and the participants reappear in of or by phrases. It compresses information and hides the agent.

  • The government decided to intervene. → the government’s decision to intervene
  • Prices rose sharply. → the sharp rise in prices — adverb becomes adjective
  • We analysed the data carefully. → careful analysis of the data — agent removed
  • Fire destroyed the city. → the destruction of the city by fire — by keeps the agent
Teaching sequence
1
Verb/adjective → noun

Turn processes into nouns for an abstract, objective register: 'decide → the decision to', 'fail → the failure of'.

Support : 'reduce costs → the reduction of costs'

2
Information density

Rewrite verbal sentences as nominal ones, noting the more formal, compact effect. Drill on academic extracts.

3
Production

Reformulate an informal paragraph into nominalised academic prose.

Common pitfall : Over-nominalising buries the action and produces heavy, lifeless prose; alternate with strong verbs to keep the writing readable.

Verbs & modes

Inversion after negative adverbials (Never have I...) C140 questions

Grammar note

When a negative or restrictive adverbial is fronted, the clause takes question word order: adverbial + auxiliary + subject + main verb. If there is no auxiliary, supply do, does or did. Triggers include never, rarely, seldom, little, hardly, no sooner, not only, under no circumstances and only + time phrase.

  • Never have I heard such nonsense. — auxiliary placed before the subject
  • Rarely does he arrive on time. — do-support with a simple tense
  • Not only did she apologise, but she also paid for the damage.
  • Only after the meeting did we understand the plan. — only + adverbial
Teaching sequence
1
Fronting triggers inversion

Front a negative/limiting adverbial and invert subject and auxiliary: 'Never have I seen such a thing. / Not only did he lie, but...'.

Support : 'Rarely do we see this.'

2
Common triggers

Drill the set: never, rarely, seldom, hardly...when, no sooner...than, not only, little, only then. Transform from neutral sentences.

3
Production

Rewrite emphatic statements with fronted negatives + inversion.

Common pitfall : Inversion is obligatory once the negative adverbial is fronted: 'Never have I...' (not 'Never I have...'). Keep it for formal/rhetorical effect.

Subjunctive & 'were to' / 'be to' C140 questions

Grammar note

The mandative subjunctive uses the bare base form in a that-clause after verbs and adjectives of demand or advice (insist, recommend, essential): I insist that he be told; negative: that he not be told. Were is the past subjunctive of be in unreal conditions, and were to + infinitive marks a remote hypothesis.

  • The board recommends that the report be published. — base form, no -s
  • It is vital that she not miss the deadline. — negative subjunctive
  • If I were you, I would say nothing. — were with every person
  • Were the company to relocate, hundreds would lose their jobs. — inverted were to
Teaching sequence
1
Mandative subjunctive

Base verb after demand/suggest/insist + that: 'I suggest that he be informed. / It is essential that she arrive on time.'

Support : 'They demanded that he leave.'

2
Hypothetical 'were to' / planning 'be to'

'If I were to tell you...' (tentative future); 'be to' for official plans/instructions ('You are to report at 9'). Contrast uses.

3
Production

Write formal recommendations and tentative hypotheses using these forms.

Common pitfall : The mandative subjunctive uses the BASE verb with no -s ('that he be', 'that she arrive'); BrE may use 'should + verb' instead.

Future in the past C140 questions

Grammar note

To look forward from a point in the past, backshift the future forms: will → would, is going to → was/were going to, is about to → was about to, is to → was to. Was going to and was about to often imply the event never happened, while was to reports destiny seen in hindsight.

  • She said she would call me back. — will becomes would
  • We were going to leave early, but the meeting overran. — plan not carried out
  • He was about to speak when the lights went out. — imminence in the past
  • They didn’t know it, but he was to become their finest player. — hindsight
Teaching sequence
1
Past viewpoint, later action

From a past moment, refer to something then still future: 'was going to, would, was about to, was to.' 'He was going to call but forgot.'

Support : 'She was to become president.'

2
Choose the right form

Match nuance: intention (was going to), destiny (was to), imminence (was about to). Sorting drill.

3
Production

Narrate past plans and how they turned out.

Common pitfall : Backshift 'will' to 'would' inside past narration: 'He said he would help' / 'I knew it would rain' (not 'will' in a past frame).

Modal nuances (habits, willingness, criticism) C140 questions

Grammar note

Beyond permission and possibility, modals carry attitude: would and used to for past habits; will and would for typical or irritating behaviour (He will interrupt); will and would for willingness or refusal (The door won’t open); should have, could have and might have + past participle for criticism or regret about the past.

  • Every summer we would drive to the coast. — repeated past habit
  • He will leave his cup on my desk. — irritating characteristic behaviour
  • The car wouldn’t start this morning. — refusal, inanimate subject
  • You might have told me the meeting was cancelled. — reproach about the past
Teaching sequence
1
Beyond core meanings

'will/would' for typical behaviour ('He will sit there for hours'); 'would' for past willingness/refusal ('The car wouldn't start'); 'might/could have' for criticism ('You could have told me').

Support : 'She would always help.'

2
Read the context

Identify the nuance (habit, willingness, reproach, annoyance) from context. Analysis of authentic lines.

3
Production

Express criticism and characteristic behaviour using these modal nuances.

Common pitfall : 'You should have told me' / 'You could have warned me' carry reproach about the past; learners often miss this critical, non-literal nuance.

Advanced aspect (perfect vs simple contrasts) C140 questions

Grammar note

Perfect forms are relational: they place an event before a reference point and make it relevant there (present perfect — before now; past perfect — before a past point). Simple forms simply locate a complete event on the timeline. Choose the perfect when the connection matters, the simple when the time or the sequence does.

  • I’ve lost my keys. — result matters now, the time is irrelevant
  • I lost my keys on Tuesday. — event anchored to a past time
  • By the time we arrived, the film had started. — anterior to a past point
  • She has been living here since March. — perfect continuous: duration up to now
Teaching sequence
1
Subtle tense choices

Fine-tune present perfect vs past simple, and simple vs continuous, for connotation: finished vs relevant-now, permanent vs temporary, complete vs in progress.

Support : 'I lived there' vs 'I have lived there' vs 'I have been living there'

2
Connotation drills

Compare near-minimal pairs and articulate the difference in meaning/emphasis. Discussion-based analysis.

3
Production

Write a short reflective text exploiting aspectual contrasts deliberately.

Common pitfall : The choice is about viewpoint, not just time: 'What have you done?' (result now) vs 'What did you do?' (past event) can both be 'correct' but mean different things.

Adjectives

Adjective complementation (keen on, eager to) C140 questions

Grammar note

Many adjectives require a fixed complement. Some take a preposition + noun or -ing (keen on sailing, good at maths, afraid of falling), some take a to-infinitive (eager to help, likely to rain, easy to use), and some take a that-clause (glad that you came). The pattern belongs to the adjective and is learnt with it.

  • She is keen on cycling. — preposition + -ing form
  • He was eager to start. — to-infinitive complement
  • I’m afraid of flying, but afraid to tell anyone. — of + -ing versus to + infinitive
  • We are aware that the deadline has moved. — that-clause complement
Teaching sequence
1
Adjective + preposition / clause

Many adjectives take a fixed preposition or pattern: 'keen on -ing, eager to do, aware that, capable of -ing.' Group by pattern.

Support : 'afraid of failing', 'likely to win'

2
Preposition vs infinitive

Contrast adjectives taking 'of + -ing' vs 'to + infinitive'. Drill collocations.

3
Production

Describe attitudes and abilities using correctly complemented adjectives.

Common pitfall : The preposition is fixed and not always logical: 'interested in', 'good at', 'responsible for' — learn them as collocations, not by rule.

Pronouns & reference

Cohesion & substitution (do so, such) C140 questions

Grammar note

Substitution replaces a repeated element with a shorter pro-form: one and ones for a count noun, do so or do that for a whole verb phrase, so or not after think, hope and expect, and such (a) for a previously mentioned kind. The text stays short while the link back to the earlier idea is preserved.

  • I need a pen. Have you got one? — one replaces a count noun
  • He was asked to resign, and he did so at once. — do so replaces a verb phrase
  • Is she coming? I hope so. I hope not. — so and not replace a clause
  • The delays were serious; such problems are common in winter. — such refers to a kind
Teaching sequence
1
Avoid repetition

Use substitution to refer back: 'do so / do it / do that' for verb phrases; 'such' and 'so' for ideas. 'I asked him to leave and he did so.'

Support : 'She was angry, and understandably so.'

2
Register of 'do so'

'do so' is formal; 'do it/that' more neutral. Drill substitution in connected text.

3
Production

Edit a repetitive paragraph using substitution devices.

Common pitfall : Substitutes need a clear, recoverable antecedent; an unanchored 'do so' or 'such' leaves the reader guessing what is referred to.

Ellipsis (auxiliary & to-infinitive) C140 questions

Grammar note

Ellipsis omits repeated words the listener can recover. After an auxiliary, drop everything that follows but keep the auxiliary itself (She hasn’t finished, but he has). After a verb taking a to-infinitive, keep the bare to (I wanted to). In coordination, the repeated subject or verb can also be dropped.

  • Anna can drive, but her brother can’t. — auxiliary kept, verb phrase dropped
  • I didn’t apply, though I wanted to. — bare to retained as the trace
  • Have you finished? Yes, I have. — short answer built on ellipsis
  • She ordered the soup and he the salad. — gapping: the verb is omitted
Teaching sequence
1
Leave out the recoverable

Omit repeated words after auxiliaries or 'to': 'I haven't finished but she has (finished). / I'd love to (come).'

Support : 'You can go if you want to.'

2
Coordination ellipsis

Drop repeated subjects/verbs in coordination ('She bought the tickets and (she) booked the hotel'). Drill restoration.

3
Production

Tighten a wordy text by ellipting recoverable elements.

Common pitfall : Keep the stranded auxiliary or 'to' as the trace: 'I want to' (not 'I want'); 'she has' (not 'she has finished' repeated, but also not bare 'she').

Adverbs & modality

Epistemic adverbs (apparently, presumably) C140 questions

Grammar note

Epistemic adverbs show how certain you are and where your information comes from. High certainty: certainly, clearly, undoubtedly. Inference: presumably, evidently, seemingly. Hearsay: apparently, reportedly, allegedly. Low certainty: possibly, perhaps. They stand at the start of the clause or just before the main verb.

  • Apparently, the flight has been cancelled. — hearsay, not first-hand
  • She has presumably read the report by now. — inference, before the main verb
  • The scheme will undoubtedly cost more than planned. — high certainty
  • Perhaps we should wait a little longer. — low certainty, initial position
Teaching sequence
1
Mark your certainty

Signal how sure you are: certainly, presumably, apparently, supposedly, allegedly (decreasing certainty/distance). Place on a scale.

Support : 'Apparently, they have split up.'

2
Source distancing

'allegedly/supposedly/reportedly' distance you from a claim you don't fully endorse. Drill in reporting contexts.

3
Production

Report unverified news, calibrating commitment with the right adverb.

Common pitfall : 'supposedly/allegedly' signal doubt or hearsay, not neutral fact; using them carelessly implies you don't believe the claim.

Comment & viewpoint adverbs (frankly, technically) C140 questions

Grammar note

A sentence adverb modifies the whole clause, not the verb. Comment adverbs (frankly, obviously, unfortunately) give the speaker's attitude and unpack as “to be frank”, “it is obvious that”; viewpoint adverbs (technically, financially, politically) limit the claim to one domain. Both usually stand first, marked off by a comma.

  • Frankly, I don't believe a word of it. — comment: “to be frank”
  • Technically, the contract is still valid. — viewpoint: in technical terms
  • She has, unfortunately, declined the offer. — mid-position, commas both sides
  • He answered frankly. — manner, no comma: how he answered
Teaching sequence
1
Comment on the whole clause

Sentence adverbs frame the statement: 'Frankly, I disagree. / Technically, it's illegal. / Unfortunately, we lost.'

Support : 'Personally, I'd refuse.'

2
Position & punctuation

Usually sentence-initial with a comma. Distinguish comment adverbs (frankly = I speak frankly) from manner adverbs (he spoke frankly).

3
Production

Add stance to statements using comment and viewpoint adverbs.

Common pitfall : A comment adverb judges the whole sentence, not the verb: 'Clearly, he lied' (it is clear that) differs from 'He spoke clearly' (manner).

Syntax & discourse

Fronting & emphasis C140 questions

Grammar note

Fronting moves a non-subject element to the head of the clause for emphasis or cohesion. Fronted objects, complements and ordinary adverbials keep normal word order. A fronted negative or restrictive adverbial (never, rarely, seldom, little, not only, only then) forces auxiliary inversion, with do-support if there is no auxiliary.

  • That kind of behaviour I will not tolerate. — fronted object, no inversion
  • Never had I seen such a mess. — negative adverbial, auxiliary before subject
  • Not only did he apologise, he paid for the damage. — do-support required
  • Down the hill rolled the barrel. — place adverbial, full-verb inversion
Teaching sequence
1
Move an element to the front

Front objects, complements or adverbials for emphasis/cohesion: 'That I can't accept. / Down the road came a car.'

Support : 'Such was his anger that he left.'

2
With and without inversion

Some fronting triggers inversion ('Down came the rain'), some doesn't ('This book I love'). Sort examples.

3
Production

Rewrite neutral sentences with marked fronting for effect.

Common pitfall : Fronting is a stylistic, marked choice for cohesion or emphasis; overusing it sounds contrived, and some patterns require inversion.

Cleft & pseudo-cleft (advanced) C140 questions

Grammar note

A cleft splits one clause in two to spotlight a single element. It-cleft: It + be + focus + that/who clause. Wh-cleft: What + clause + be + focus, which puts the focus at the end. Reversed wh-clefts and the all-cleft work the same way; whatever is left outside the focus is presented as already known.

  • It was in 2019 that the firm collapsed. — it-cleft focusing an adverbial
  • What surprised us was his calm. — wh-cleft, focus in final position
  • That is what I meant. — reversed wh-cleft
  • All I asked for was an apology. — all-cleft, exclusive focus
Teaching sequence
1
Range of clefts

Beyond basic clefts: 'All I want is..., The reason (why)..., The place where..., What happened was...'. Mark the focus precisely.

Support : 'The thing that bothers me is the cost.'

2
Choose the structure for focus

Match cleft type to the element emphasised and the discourse flow. Transformation drills.

3
Production

Restructure an argument's key points with varied clefts.

Common pitfall : Clefts manage given/new information; piling them up or choosing the wrong type disrupts the natural flow rather than aiding emphasis.

Discourse markers (moreover, nevertheless) C140 questions

Grammar note

Discourse markers are adverbials, not conjunctions: they link a sentence to what came before and are set off by a comma, after a full stop or a semicolon. Choose one by the relation you mean — addition (moreover, furthermore), contrast (however, nevertheless), result (hence, consequently), concession (admittedly), reformulation (that is).

  • The costs are high; moreover, the timetable is unrealistic. — addition
  • She had little experience. Nevertheless, she was appointed. — concessive contrast
  • Demand collapsed; consequently, prices fell. — result
  • The results were, however, inconclusive. — mid-position, commas both sides
Teaching sequence
1
Formal cohesion set

Organise written argument with moreover, furthermore, nevertheless, conversely, hence, thereby, whereas. Group by function (add/contrast/result).

Support : 'The plan is costly; nevertheless, it is necessary.'

2
Register & precision

Distinguish near-synonyms (however vs nevertheless vs nonetheless) and avoid spoken markers in formal writing. Drill selection.

3
Production

Structure a formal argumentative paragraph with precise markers.

Common pitfall : Markers must reflect a genuine logical relation; a string of impressive connectives without real links between ideas weakens the argument.

Hedging & academic register C140 questions

Grammar note

Hedging weakens a claim so that it can still be defended. The main devices are modals (may, might, could), reporting verbs (suggest, indicate, appear, tend to), adverbs (arguably, largely, relatively), approximators (some, around) and impersonal frames (It could be argued that). One hedge per claim is normally enough.

  • The data suggest that literacy rates may have improved. — verb plus modal
  • The effect appears to be largely attributable to sampling. — appear plus adverb
  • It could be argued that the model is oversimplified. — impersonal frame
  • Around a third of respondents disagreed. — approximator softens a figure
Teaching sequence
1
Soften claims

Use hedges for cautious, academic tone: 'may/might, tend to, it appears that, to some extent, arguably.' 'This may suggest that...'.

Support : 'The data appear to indicate a trend.'

2
Balance hedge & stance

Combine hedging with cautious commitment; avoid both overclaiming and excessive vagueness. Edit overstated sentences.

3
Production

Rewrite bold assertions as appropriately hedged academic claims.

Common pitfall : Hedging shows academic caution, but too many hedges ('it might perhaps possibly suggest') make the writing evasive and weak — one hedge is usually enough.

C2 C2 — Mastery

Absolute precision · style · variation · metalanguage

Nouns & stylistics

Genitive & possession nuances (of vs 's; double genitive) C240 questions

Grammar note

Use 's with people, animals, groups and time or measure phrases (the manager's decision, a day's work); use of with inanimate things and long noun phrases (the roof of the building). The double genitive — a, that or some + noun + of + 's form or possessive pronoun — selects one item from a set.

  • a friend of my sister's — one of several friends she has
  • the leg of the table — inanimate, of-genitive preferred
  • the King of Spain's visit — group genitive: 's on the whole phrase
  • I stayed at my aunt's. — ellipted genitive, house understood
Teaching sequence
1
Choose 's or of

's for living/personified possessors and time ('a day's work'); 'of' for inanimate/abstract and long phrases. Compare nuance.

Support : 'the company's policy' vs 'the policy of the company'

2
Double genitive

'a friend of mine', 'a painting of Picasso's' (one of his works) vs 'a painting of Picasso' (depicting him). Disambiguation drill.

3
Production

Edit phrases choosing the genitive that fits sense and rhythm.

Common pitfall : 'a portrait of the king' (it depicts him) differs from 'a portrait of the king's' (one he owns/painted) — the double genitive changes the meaning.

Marked noun forms (foreign plurals: criteria, phenomena) C240 questions

Grammar note

Learned nouns from Latin and Greek keep their source plurals in careful use: -on gives -a (criterion, criteria; phenomenon, phenomena), -um gives -a (datum, data; medium, media), -us gives -i (stimulus, stimuli), -is gives -es (analysis, analyses), -ex/-ix gives -ices (index, indices). Some also have a regular plural with a different sense.

  • one criterion, two criteria — singular in -on, plural in -a
  • These analyses confirm the earlier hypotheses. — -is becomes -es
  • The stimuli were presented in random order. — -us becomes -i
  • indices of a book's quality / indexes at the back — split plural, split meaning
Teaching sequence
1
Latin/Greek plurals

criterion/criteria, phenomenon/phenomena, datum/data, analysis/analyses, index/indices. Treat as pairs to master.

Support : 'These criteria are...' (plural)

2
Shifting usage

Note items drifting to regular usage ('data' as singular mass noun in many fields). Discuss register and field norms.

3
Production

Use these nouns with correct agreement in academic sentences.

Common pitfall : 'criteria/phenomena/data' are plural in careful use ('one criterion', 'these phenomena'); 'a criteria' or 'this phenomena' is a marked error in formal writing.

Verbs — full mastery

All tenses & aspects (controlled production) C240 questions

Grammar note

English crosses two tenses (present, past) with two aspects (perfect, progressive), giving twelve active forms. The perfect marks anteriority and relevance to a reference point; the progressive presents the event as ongoing, temporary or incomplete; the simple form views it as a whole. Keep one reference time across a passage.

  • By June we had been testing the prototype for a year. — duration up to a past point
  • She has lived here since 2010. — present perfect: still true now
  • I was reading when the lights went out. — progressive frames a past simple event
  • Water boils at 100 degrees Celsius. — present simple for a timeless truth
Teaching sequence
1
The whole system

Produce the full English tense-aspect grid accurately, including perfect continuous and future perfect, choosing each for precise effect.

Support : 'By then I will have been working here for a decade.'

2
Recognise rare/literary forms

Read texts with marked forms ('be to', historic present, narrative inversion) and gloss their effect.

3
Production

Write a passage that deliberately deploys a wide, precise range of tenses.

Common pitfall : At this level the issue is precise CHOICE and consistent sequence of tense, not formation; a single backshift slip or aspect mismatch betrays the level.

Rare/literary structures (inversion in conditionals) C240 questions

Grammar note

In formal or literary style if can be dropped and the clause inverted, but only with had, were and should. Had + subject + past participle equals if + had; Were + subject (+ to + infinitive) equals if + were (to); Should + subject + bare infinitive marks a remote possibility. The negative is never contracted.

  • Had I known, I would have said nothing. — replaces If I had known
  • Were she to resign, the board would collapse. — replaces If she were to resign
  • Should you need help, call this number. — tentative future condition
  • Had it not been for your warning, we would have failed. — *Hadn't it been for
Teaching sequence
1
Conditional without 'if'

Invert to drop 'if' in formal/literary style: 'Had I known... / Were she to refuse... / Should you need help...'.

Support : 'Had they arrived earlier, all would have been well.'

2
Map to standard conditionals

Rewrite each inverted form as the equivalent if-clause and back. Register awareness.

3
Production

Use inverted conditionals appropriately in formal writing.

Common pitfall : Inverted conditionals are formal and structure-specific: 'Had I known' (not 'Had I have known'); 'Were I you' (not 'Was I you').

Tense shifts in narrative & backshift subtleties C240 questions

Grammar note

In reported speech backshift is optional when the content still holds or the report is immediate. Unreal past forms and the modals would, could, might and should do not shift again. In narrative, the past perfect marks a flashback and only the first verbs need it; switching to the present tense makes an episode vivid.

  • She told me she is Portuguese. — permanent fact, no backshift needed
  • He said he would have finished by then. — would is already unreal, no further shift
  • He had been sacked; that was why he left. — past perfect frames the earlier event
  • So there I am, standing in the rain. — historic present in spoken narrative
Teaching sequence
1
Manage the timeline

Coordinate past simple, past perfect and reported forms across a long narrative; use the historic present for vividness.

Support : 'He had been waiting for hours when, suddenly, the door opens.'

2
Optional backshift

Note when backshift is optional (still-true facts: 'She said the Earth is/was round') and how it signals stance. Analysis.

3
Production

Write/edit a narrative with controlled, purposeful tense shifts.

Common pitfall : Backshift is sometimes optional for general truths or current relevance ('he said he is tired' if still tired); rigidly backshifting everything can distort meaning.

Aspectual nuance (stative vs dynamic, habitual) C240 questions

Grammar note

Stative verbs (know, own, contain, resemble, believe) describe unbounded states and normally take the simple form; dynamic verbs take the progressive to show an action in progress. A stative in the progressive forces a dynamic reading: temporary behaviour or a deliberate activity. Habits take the simple present, or used to and would in the past.

  • I think it is unfair. / I am thinking about your offer. — state versus mental activity
  • She is being difficult today. — temporary behaviour, not a permanent trait
  • This bottle contains two litres. — *is containing: pure state
  • We would spend every August by the sea. — would for a past habit
Teaching sequence
1
Coerced statives

Explore stative verbs used dynamically for effect ('I'm loving this', 'You're being silly') and the meaning shift this creates.

Support : 'I'm thinking' (process) vs 'I think' (opinion)

2
Habitual & iterative shades

Contrast 'used to / would / kept -ing / will (habitual)' for nuance. Fine-grained sorting.

3
Production

Exploit aspectual coercion deliberately in a stylistic text.

Common pitfall : Continuous use of a stative verb changes its meaning ('I'm seeing someone' = dating; 'You're being rude' = behaviour now) — not a free variant of the simple form.

Adjectives — extreme cases

Adjective compounds & hyphenation C240 questions

Grammar note

Hyphenate a multi-word modifier when it stands before its noun and the words function as one unit: a well-known writer, a state-of-the-art lab, a six-month delay (the noun stays singular). The hyphen disappears when the same phrase follows the noun, and a first element in -ly never takes one.

  • a hard-working student — compound adjective before the noun
  • The student is hard working. — predicative, hyphen dropped
  • a highly motivated team — *highly-motivated: no hyphen after -ly
  • a ten-year-old boy — singular year inside the modifier
Teaching sequence
1
Form compound adjectives

Build hyphenated modifiers: 'a state-of-the-art system, a well-thought-out plan, a two-thirds majority.' Patterns: noun+participle, adverb+adjective, number+noun.

Support : 'a hard-to-please critic'

2
Hyphen rules

Hyphenate before the noun, usually not after; no hyphen after -ly adverbs ('a highly skilled worker'). Drill the conventions.

3
Production

Write descriptions using precise compound adjectives.

Common pitfall : No hyphen after an -ly adverb ('a beautifully written essay', not 'beautifully-written') and none in predicative position ('the plan was well thought out').

Adjectives in fixed expressions & idioms C240 questions

Grammar note

Many adjectives are locked into fixed strings whose wording and order cannot be altered: binomials (safe and sound, short and sweet), intensifying collocations chosen by the noun (heavy rain, strong coffee, deep sleep) and idioms (a close shave, sheer luck). Learn them whole; the usual rules of substitution and order do not apply.

  • They arrived safe and sound. — frozen binomial, shorter word first
  • We had a narrow escape. — *a thin escape: the collocation is fixed
  • It was sheer luck. — sheer only before the noun, never after be
  • heavy rain but strong wind — each noun selects its own intensifier
Teaching sequence
1
Set collocations

Master fixed adjective phrases: 'a foregone conclusion, a close call, common ground, a fine line, safe and sound.' Treat as wholes.

Support : 'It's a foregone conclusion.'

2
Binomials

Drill fixed-order pairs ('safe and sound', 'short and sweet', 'sick and tired') whose order can't be reversed.

3
Production

Weave fixed adjective expressions naturally into discourse.

Common pitfall : Fixed binomials have a frozen order: 'safe and sound' (never 'sound and safe'); changing it sounds non-native even though the meaning is clear.

Pronouns — marginal cases

Generic & impersonal 'one' / 'you' / 'they' C240 questions

Grammar note

Three impersonal subjects state a general truth. One is formal and includes the speaker, with the forms one's and oneself. You is its informal spoken equivalent and addresses nobody in particular. They points to unidentified people or authorities. A passive or a general noun avoids all three.

  • One cannot be too careful. — formal generic, speaker included
  • You never know what may happen. — informal generic in speech
  • They are putting the fares up again. — unidentified authorities
  • One should not judge others by oneself. — one's and oneself match one
Teaching sequence
1
Three impersonals

'one' (formal generic: 'One should be careful'), 'you' (informal generic), 'they' (people/authorities unspecified: 'They say...'). Compare register.

Support : 'You never know.' / 'One never knows.'

2
Consistency

Keep the chosen pronoun consistent ('one... one's...', not switching to 'you'). Drill register-appropriate choice.

3
Production

Write the same general statement in three registers.

Common pitfall : Don't switch impersonals mid-text ('One should look after one's health', not '...your health'); 'one' is formal and can sound stiff if overused.

Logical & anticipatory 'it' C240 questions

Grammar note

Anticipatory it holds the subject or object slot while the real content is extraposed to the end, normally a that-clause or a to-infinitive: It is obvious that he lied; I find it hard to refuse. After find, think, make and consider, this it is obligatory before an adjective plus clause. Logical (empty) it also covers weather, time and distance.

  • It seems that the train has been cancelled. — anticipatory subject, that-clause extraposed
  • It is essential to book early. — anticipatory subject, to-infinitive extraposed
  • She made it clear that she disagreed. — obligatory anticipatory object after make
  • It is raining and it is nearly midnight. — logical it, no referent at all
Teaching sequence
1
Postpone heavy subjects/objects

'It' anticipates a clause: 'It is clear that... / I find it hard to believe that... / It was here that it happened.'

Support : 'It strikes me as odd that he agreed.'

2
Object 'it'

Anticipatory object: 'I owe it to you to explain / She made it clear that...'. Transformation drill.

3
Production

Restructure clause-heavy sentences using anticipatory 'it'.

Common pitfall : The anticipatory 'it' is obligatory and not a real referent: 'I find it difficult to say' (not 'I find difficult to say' or 'I find this difficult to say' in that frame).

Adverbs & style

Stylistic inversion & rhetorical syntax C240 questions

Grammar note

In formal or emphatic style, fronting a negative or restrictive adverbial (never, rarely, seldom, little, not only, no sooner, only then, under no circumstances) forces subject–operator inversion: Only then did he realise. Fronted place adverbials invert fully with intransitive verbs, and conditionals may drop if: Had I known, Were it not for you.

  • Not only did he refuse, he laughed. — negative fronting with operator inversion
  • Should you require assistance, please ring. — inverted conditional, if omitted
  • In the doorway stood a stranger. — full inversion after a fronted place adverbial
  • *Never I had seen such a thing → Never had I seen such a thing
Teaching sequence
1
Marked word order for effect

Deploy rhetorical inversion and balanced/periodic sentences: 'So intense was the heat that... / Not until later did we realise...'.

Support : 'Such is life.'

2
Parallelism & antithesis

Build parallel and antithetical structures for rhetorical force ('Not that I loved Caesar less, but that I loved Rome more').

3
Production

Craft a persuasive passage using inversion and balanced syntax.

Common pitfall : Rhetorical inversion is high-register and purposeful; misjudged, it reads as pompous or archaic rather than emphatic.

Pragmatics: irony, implicature, understatement C240 questions

Grammar note

Speakers mean more than they say. Understatement and litotes soften or intensify by negating the opposite (not bad, hardly ideal, a bit of a problem); irony conveys the reverse of the literal wording, signalled by stress, exaggeration or clash with context. Implicature is inferred, so the surface form stays literally polite.

  • That went well. — ironic, said after an obvious failure
  • She is not unintelligent. — litotes: guarded praise
  • It is a little chilly today. — understatement for minus ten degrees
  • I have read some of the report. — implicature: not all of it
Teaching sequence
1
Meaning beyond words

Decode and produce irony, conversational implicature (Grice) and British understatement ('not bad' = very good).

Support : 'That's not exactly cheap.' (= it's expensive)

2
Implied, not stated

Analyse what is implicated rather than asserted ('Some passed' implies not all). Inference practice.

3
Production

Convey criticism or praise indirectly through understatement and irony.

Common pitfall : Irony and understatement are culture-bound; an implicature obvious to a native may be missed or taken literally by another audience — calibrate to listeners.

Syntax & metalanguage

Grammatical metalanguage C240 questions

Grammar note

Precise description separates three questions: word class (noun, verb, determiner, preposition), function in the clause (subject, object, complement, adjunct) and form (finite or non-finite, active or passive, count or uncount). One item carries a label on each level at once, and a single class can fill several functions.

  • In Her plan surprised us, plan is a noun (class) and head of the subject (function).
  • Running exhausts me. — running: non-finite -ing form functioning as subject
  • He painted the door blue. — blue is an object complement, not an adverbial
  • That she left early annoyed him. — a finite that-clause in subject function
Teaching sequence
1
Vocabulary to analyse language

Master terms to describe and correct English: clause, complement, modifier, aspect, transitivity, subordination, anaphora. Apply to sample sentences.

Support : 'identify the subordinate clause and its function'

2
Analyse & self-correct

Parse complex sentences and explain errors using precise terminology. Peer-correction tasks.

3
Production

Annotate a text, labelling structures and justifying corrections.

Common pitfall : Metalanguage should clarify, not obscure: use the precise term where it aids analysis, but explaining a point in plain English is often more effective.

Register & variation (BrE/AmE, formal/informal) C240 questions

Grammar note

Two axes vary independently. Variety: colour/color, centre/center, travelled/traveled, have you got/do you have, gotten only in AmE, at the weekend/on the weekend, The team are/The team is. Formality: contractions, phrasal verbs and get belong to informal registers; full forms, Latinate verbs and nominalisation to formal ones.

  • I have just eaten. (BrE) / I just ate. (AmE) — present perfect versus past simple
  • Have you got a pen? (BrE) / Do you have a pen? (AmE)
  • We put the meeting off. (informal) / We postponed the meeting. (formal)
  • The government are divided. (BrE, notional plural) / The government is divided. (AmE)
Teaching sequence
1
Systematic differences

Compare BrE/AmE grammar (have got vs have, present perfect use, collective agreement, 'at the weekend' vs 'on the weekend') and formal/informal choices.

Support : 'I've just eaten' (BrE) vs 'I just ate' (AmE)

2
Choose for context

Select the variety/register a task or client expects. Sorting and rewriting across registers.

3
Production

Write one message in two varieties/registers for different audiences.

Common pitfall : The error is inconsistency — mixing BrE and AmE spelling/grammar in one document. Pick the variety the context demands and apply it throughout.

Disambiguating complex sentences C240 questions

Grammar note

Most ambiguity comes from attachment (which head a phrase modifies), scope (negation, quantifiers, coordination) and unclear reference. Repair it structurally rather than by explanation: put the modifier beside its head, add a comma or a relative pronoun, repeat the determiner or preposition across coordinated items, or split the sentence.

  • She saw the man with the telescope. → Using the telescope, she saw the man. — attachment
  • old men and women → old men and old women — scope of the adjective made explicit
  • The students, who had revised, passed. — commas: all of them; no commas: only some
  • All the seats were not taken. → Not all the seats were taken. — scope of negation
Teaching sequence
1
Spot the ambiguity

Locate scope, attachment and reference ambiguities: 'He told her he loved her at the party' (where?), negation scope, dangling modifiers.

Support : 'Visiting relatives can be tiring.' (who visits?)

2
Rewrite for one reading

Reorder, repunctuate, add a pronoun or split the sentence to fix a single meaning without losing nuance. Editing drill.

3
Production

Revise an ambiguous text so each sentence has one clear reading.

Common pitfall : Disambiguation shouldn't bloat the sentence: aim for the simplest version with only one reading — sometimes two short sentences beat one clarified monster.

Formal written rhetoric & cohesion C240 questions

Grammar note

Cohesion in formal prose rests on reference (this argument, the latter, such a view), connectors that name the logical relation (however, consequently, by contrast, admittedly) and information order: given material first, new and heavy material last. Passives, clefts and fronting exist to preserve that given-to-new flow.

  • The data were incomplete; consequently, the conclusions remain provisional.
  • Two objections arise. The former is empirical, the latter conceptual. — cohesive reference
  • It was the sample size, not the method, that skewed the results. — cleft for focus
  • Such an approach has since been abandoned. — summarising noun phrase linking back
Teaching sequence
1
Structure an argument

Plan and signpost extended argument: thesis, development, counter-argument, synthesis, with cohesive ties across paragraphs.

Support : 'It follows, therefore, that...'

2
Cohesion & flow

Manage theme/rheme progression, reference chains and transitions for seamless flow. Analyse a model essay.

3
Production

Write a cohesive argumentative essay with controlled rhetorical structure.

Common pitfall : Cohesive devices and rhetoric serve the logic of ideas; elegant connectors can't rescue an argument whose underlying reasoning doesn't progress.
